Infinite-Dimensional Stochastic Differential Equations and Diffusion Dynamics of Coulomb Random Point Fields
Hirofumi Osada ∗ , Shota Osada †

Abstract
. We consider the stochastic dynamics of infinite particle systems in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} interacting through the d d -dimensional Coulomb potential. For arbitrary inverse temperature β > 0 \beta>0 and all dimensions d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , we construct solutions to the associated infinite-dimensional stochastic differential equations (ISDEs). Our main results establish the existence of strong solutions and their pathwise uniqueness. The resulting labeled process is an ( ℝ d ) ℕ (\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}} -valued diffusion with no invariant measure, while the associated unlabeled process is reversible with respect to the Coulomb random point field. Furthermore, we prove that finite-particle systems converge to these infinite-particle dynamics, thereby providing a rigorous foundation for Coulomb interacting Brownian motion.



∗
Chubu University,
osada@fsc.chubu.ac.jp

†
Kagoshima University,
s-osada@edu.kagoshima-u.ac.jp

Keyword:

Coulomb random point field

infinite-dimensional stochastic differential equationinfinite-particle system

Acknowledgements:
This work was supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant Numbers JP21H04432, 22K13931

1.
Introduction.

The Coulomb potential occupies a central position in mathematics and the natural sciences.
For
d\geq 2
, define
\Psi\!:\!\mathbb{R}^{d}\!\to\!\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
by

\displaystyle\Psi({x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d-2}\frac{1}{|{x}|^{d-2}},\quad\text{ for }d\geq 3,

\displaystyle=-\log\lvert{x}\rvert,\quad\text{ for }d=2.

(1.1)

Then the gradient of
\Psi
has the simple form

\displaystyle\nabla\Psi({x})=-\frac{{x}}{|{x}|^{d}}.

(1.2)

We call
\Psi
the
d
-dimensional Coulomb potential. The object of this paper is to construct the stochastic dynamics of infinite-particle systems in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
interacting through
\Psi
at inverse temperature
\beta>0
. These dynamics are expected to satisfy the infinite-dimensional stochastic differential equation (ISDE)

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=B_{t}^{i}+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert<R,\ j\neq i}\frac{X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}}{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}|^{d}}du\quad(i\in\mathbb{N}),

(1.3)

which we call the
Coulomb interacting Brownian motion
.

A striking feature of this problem is the exact correspondence between the ambient spatial dimension
d
and the exponent in the Coulomb potential. This coincidence is not accidental: it is the source of both the depth and the difficulty of the problem. On the one hand, it ensures that Coulomb random point fields are natural equilibrium distributions in any dimension
d\geq 2
; on the other hand, it makes the analysis of the associated ISDE (
1.3
) extremely delicate, due to the long-range nature and singularity of the Coulomb force.

To date, the only case where (
1.3
) has been solved is
d=\beta=2
, corresponding to the Ginibre random point field. In this special situation, the determinantal structure of the equilibrium measure provides powerful tools, including sharp hyperuniform estimates for particle number fluctuations. Beyond
d=\beta=2
, however, such determinantal methods break down, and no general construction of the dynamics has been available.

From the equilibrium viewpoint, the classical Gibbsian framework excludes Coulomb interactions because the potential is neither superstable nor integrable at infinity. The construction of Coulomb random point fields remained open for decades, until very recently it was resolved in general by Armstrong–Serfaty and Thoma
[
1
,
44
]
. These works establish the existence of Coulomb random point fields for all
d\geq 2
and
\beta>0
. The present paper addresses the complementary problem: the construction of the corresponding stochastic dynamics, i.e., reversible diffusions with respect to these measures.

Our approach is new, conceptually simple, and robust. For the first time, it provides a
pathwise unique strong solution

\mathbf{X}
of the Coulomb ISDE (
1.3
) for all spatial dimensions
d\geq 2
and all inverse temperatures
\beta>0
. The central innovation of the method is the introduction of a new family of analytic estimates for the logarithmic derivatives of Coulomb random point fields. These estimates replace the determinantal and random matrix arguments that were previously available only in the Ginibre case. As a consequence, the solution
\mathbf{X}
is established as an
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued diffusion process without invariant measure, while the associated unlabeled process is shown to be a reversible diffusion with respect to the Coulomb random point field.

We connect the finite ensembles to the ISDE through a two-step thermodynamic limit.
For each radius
R
, we freeze the exterior configuration on
S_{R}^{c}=\{\lvert x\rvert\geq R\}
and consider the
N
-particle gradient dynamics inside
S_{R}
with normal reflection on
\partial S_{R}
. Denoting these finite-volume systems by
\mathbf{X}_{R}^{N}
, we first take the limit
N\to\infty
at fixed
R
to obtain an infinite-particle dynamics
\mathbf{X}_{R}
in
S_{R}
(reversible for the corresponding conditional measure). We then let
R\to\infty
and prove
\mathbf{X}_{R}\Rightarrow\mathbf{X}
, where
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is the unique strong solution to the Coulomb ISDE (
1.3
).
The precise formulation of these statements, together with the assumptions required, is given in Section
2
.

In
[
28
]
, the vanishing of self-diffusion was proved for the Ginibre interacting Brownian motion.
The decisive mechanism behind this phenomenon is the number rigidity of the stationary random point field.
With the present results in place, it is natural to formulate the following conjecture:

Conjecture.

For translation-invariant Coulomb interacting Brownian motions in two dimensions, the self-diffusion coefficient vanishes at every inverse temperature
\beta>0
.
In contrast, for every
d\geq 3
, we conjecture that Coulomb interacting Brownian motions are always diffusive, i.e., their self-diffusion coefficient is strictly positive for all
\beta>0
.

We also note that Thoma
[
45
]
proved the absence of number rigidity for Coulomb random point fields for every
d\geq 3
, which strongly suggests that the self-diffusion coefficient is strictly positive in this regime.
If confirmed, this dichotomy would vividly highlight the profound role of the long-range nature of the two-dimensional Coulomb potential, and firmly place Coulomb systems within the universality paradigm of singular interacting diffusions.

Organization of the paper.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we state our main results and discuss related work and positioning.
Section
3
presents examples.
Section
4
–
5

establish the existence of the logarithmic derivative of the equilibrium measure and provide its explicit representation.
Section
6
proves non-collision estimates for solutions of ISDEs.
Section
7
–
9
introduce Dirichlet form techniques and construct weak solutions under both lower and upper schemes.
Section
10
establishes the existence of unique strong solutions, while Section
11
demonstrates the uniqueness of Dirichlet forms and completes the proof of our diffusion theorem.
Section
12
shows the convergence of finite-particle dynamics to the infinite-particle ISDE dynamics.
Finally, the Appendix (Section
13
) collects auxiliary results on configuration spaces, Dirichlet forms, and the general theory of ISDEs.

2.
Main theorems (Theorem
2.1
–Theorem
2.5
)

We now formulate the problem in detail and state our main results.

2.1.
Set up and logarithmic derivatives

Let
S_{R}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,;\,|s|<R\}
.
Define the configuration space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}=\Bigl\{\mathsf{s}\,;\,\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}},\mathsf{s}(S_{R})<\infty\quad\text{ for all }R\in\mathbb{N}\Bigr\}.

(2.1)

We equip
\mathsf{S}
with the vague topology, under which it becomes a Polish space, i.e., homeomorphic to a complete and separable metric space.

For
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, set

\mathsf{S}_{R}^{m}=\{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S};\mathsf{s}(S_{R})=m\}
.
Then, for each
R\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}=\bigsqcup_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{S}_{R}^{m}.

(2.2)

For
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{S})
, define
\pi_{A}(\mathsf{s})=\mathsf{s}(\cdot\cap A)
.
Let
S_{R}^{m}
denote the
m
-fold product of
S_{R}
.
For
\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}_{R}^{m}
, an
S_{R}^{m}
-coordinate of
\mathsf{s}
is a tuple

\mathbf{x}_{R}^{m}(\mathsf{s})\in S_{R}^{m}
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{S_{R}}(\mathsf{s})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{x_{R}^{i}(\mathsf{s})},\qquad\mathbf{x}_{R}^{m}(\mathsf{s})=(x_{R}^{i}(\mathsf{s}))_{i=1}^{m}.

Definition 2.1
.

For
f:\mathsf{S}\to\mathbb{R}
and
R,m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})
be the function defined by

\displaystyle f_{R,\cdot}^{m}:\mathsf{S}\times S_{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad(\mathsf{s},\mathbf{x})\mapsto f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x}),

(2.3)

subject to the following conditions:

\displaystyle f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})\ \text{is symmetric in $\mathbf{x}$ for each $\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}_{R}^{m}$},

(2.4)

\displaystyle f_{R,\mathsf{s}(1)}^{m}(\mathbf{x})=f_{R,\mathsf{s}(2)}^{m}(\mathbf{x})\ \text{if }\pi_{S_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s}(1))=\pi_{S_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s}(2)),

(2.5)

\displaystyle f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x}_{R}^{m}(\mathsf{s}))=f(\mathsf{s})\ \text{for }\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}_{R}^{m},

(2.6)

\displaystyle f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})=0\ \text{for }\mathsf{s}\notin\mathsf{S}_{R}^{m}.

(2.7)

Note that
f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}
is uniquely determined, and for every
\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}
,

f(\mathsf{s})=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x}_{R}^{m}(\mathsf{s})).

By convention,
\mathbf{x}_{R}^{0}(\mathsf{s})=\emptyset
for
\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}_{R}^{0}
, and
f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{0}
is constant on
\mathsf{S}_{R}^{0}
.
For a bounded set
A
, one defines
\mathbf{x}_{A}^{m}(\mathsf{s})
and
f_{A,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})
analogously by replacing
S_{R}
with
A
.

A function
f:\mathsf{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is called
smooth
if
f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})
is smooth in
\mathbf{x}
on
S_{R}^{m}
for all
R,m,\mathsf{s}
, and is called
local
if
f
is
\sigma[\pi_{S_{R}}]
-measurable for some
R\in\mathbb{N}
.
We write

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=\{f:\mathsf{S}\to\mathbb{R}\,;\,f\ \text{is $\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{S})$-measurable and smooth}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}\,;\,f\ \text{is local}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\bullet\mathrm{b}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}\,;\,f\ \text{is bounded}\},\qquad\mathscr{D}_{\circ\mathrm{b}}=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{\circ}\,;\,f\ \text{is bounded}\}.

(2.8)

Remark 2.1
.

Even if
f
is smooth, it need not be
\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{S})
-measurable. The smoothness above refers to
partial smoothness
in
\mathbf{x}
for each fixed
\mathsf{s}
.

A probability measure
\nu
on
(\mathsf{S},\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{S}))
is called a
random point field
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
A symmetric, locally integrable function
\rho_{\nu}^{m}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}
is called the
m
-point correlation function
of
\nu
(with respect to Lebesgue measure) if

\displaystyle\int_{A_{1}^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times A_{n}^{k_{n}}}\rho_{\nu}^{m}({x}^{1},\ldots,{x}^{m})\,d{x}^{1}\cdots d{x}^{m}=\int_{\mathsf{S}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathsf{s}(A_{i})!}{(\mathsf{s}(A_{i})-k_{i})!}\,\nu(d\mathsf{s}),

(2.9)

for any disjoint bounded measurable sets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
and integers
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=m
.
When
\mathsf{s}(A_{i})-k_{i}<0
, we interpret the factorial as zero.
Here,
\mathsf{s}(A)
denotes the number of particles in
A
when
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
.

For a random point field
\nu
enjoying the one-point correlation function
\rho_{\nu}^{1}
, we define
the one-reduced Campbell measure
\nu^{[1]}
of
\nu
by

\displaystyle\nu^{[1]}(dxd\mathsf{s})=\rho_{\nu}^{1}(x)\nu_{x}(d\mathsf{s})dx,

where
\nu_{x}=\nu(\cdot+\delta_{x}|\mathsf{s}(\{{x}\})\geq 1)
is the reduced Palm measure of
\nu
conditioned at
{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We set
L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(\nu^{[1]})=\bigcap_{R=1}^{\infty}L^{1}(\nu^{[1]}(\cdot\cap S_{R}\times\mathsf{S}))
.
The concept of one-reduced Campbell measure can be generalized to
m
-reduced Campbell measure and for
\nu
not necessarily enjoying factorial moments (see
[
12
]
).

We now quote the concept of the logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}
of
\nu

[
25
]
.

Definition 2.2
.

i
The logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}
of
\nu
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function such that

\mathsf{d}^{\nu}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(\nu^{[1]})
and that, for all
f\in\mathscr{D}_{\circ\mathrm{b}}^{[1]}:=C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathscr{D}_{\circ\mathrm{b}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}f(x,\mathsf{s})\mathsf{d}^{\nu}(x,\mathsf{s})\nu^{[1]}(dxd\mathsf{s})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(x,\mathsf{s})\nu^{[1]}(dxd\mathsf{s}).

(2.10)

ii
The
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}
of
\nu
is the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function defined similarly to the logarithmic derivative, but with
\mathscr{D}_{\circ\mathrm{b}}^{[1]}
replaced by
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet\mathrm{b}}^{[1]}:=C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathscr{D}_{\bullet\mathrm{b}}
.

Remark 2.2
.

A
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative is a logarithmic derivative because
\mathscr{D}_{\circ\mathrm{b}}^{[1]}\subset\mathscr{D}_{\bullet\mathrm{b}}^{[1]}
.
Both logarithmic derivatives coincide, which follows from Lemma
5.9
.

Intuitively, the logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}({x},\mathsf{s})
represents the force received by the tagged particle at
x
from (infinitely many) other particles
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
.
Informally,
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}
is defined as the differential of the logarithm of the Hamiltonian if the potential gives the equilibrium state
\nu
.
One can easily calculate the logarithmic derivatives of canonical Gibbs measures with Ruelle’s class potentials using the DLR equation
[
25
]
.
Even if the DLR equation does not hold,
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}({x},\mathsf{s})
can be well defined—this is the case for the Coulomb potentials.

The logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}
of
\nu
plays a vital role because, when it exists, it allows us to derive the stochastic dynamics of an infinite-particle system with the reversible equilibrium distribution
\nu
. This connection leads to the formulation of solutions through ISDEs, underscoring the power of this mathematical concept in understanding complex systems
[
25
,
26
,
37
]
:

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{i}=dB_{t}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}^{\nu}(X_{t}^{i},X_{t}^{i\diamond})dt\quad(i\in\mathbb{N}),

(2.11)

where
X_{t}^{i\diamond}=\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{X_{t}^{j}}
.

In
[
25
]
, it was proved that if
\nu
has a logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nu}
and the quasi-Gibbs property, then ISDE (
2.11
) has a weak solution and the associated unlabeled dynamics
\mathsf{X}_{t}=\sum\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}
is a
\nu
-reversible diffusion.
Here, the quasi-Gibbs property is a concept such that, loosely speaking, on each bounded domain, the regular conditional probability
\mu(\cdot|\mathsf{s}(S_{R})=m,\pi_{S_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s})=\pi_{S_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{y}))
of
\mu
has a local density with upper and lower inequalities to positive functions presented by given potentials
[
26
]
.
The quasi-Gibbs property is a generalization of the concept of the DLR equation and applies to Coulomb and Riesz potentials.

These two properties are satisfied by random point fields with logarithmic interaction potentials such as the Ginibre,
sin
β
, Airy
β
,
\beta=1,2,4
, and Bessel random point fields
[
25
,
26
,
27
,
35
,
10
]
.
The second author proved that, for any random point fields, only the existence of a logarithmic derivative is sufficient for constructing such dynamics
[
37
]
.
The construction of stochastic dynamics using logarithmic derivatives is universal for reversible dynamics that arise from general random point fields with Ruelle’s class potentials and random matrix theory.

The existence of the logarithmic derivative does not guarantee the uniqueness of solutions for ISDEs, a matter that remains unresolved. The resulting stochastic dynamics cannot be fully understood without more detailed information about the logarithmic derivative. Therefore, obtaining a clear and explicit representation of the logarithmic derivative is essential.

If the logarithmic derivative has an explicit expression, we can prove that the solution to (
2.11
) is a pathwise unique, strong solution
[
34
,
16
]
.
This paper proves the existence of the logarithmic derivative for the Coulomb random point field
\mu
, obtains an explicit expression to establish the uniqueness of the strong solution, and investigates other properties of stochastic dynamics.
Our method is novel and robust. It can apply to a broad class of random point fields with potentials beyond the Coulomb potentials.

2.2.
Assumptions

We begin by introducing the assumptions used throughout the paper. For
\epsilon>0
, set

\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon}^{2d}=\{({x},{y})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2};|{x}-{y}|>\epsilon\}
.

(
A1
)

Let
\{O_{N}\}
be an increasing sequence of open sets with
\bigcup_{N}O_{N}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

i
For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, assume
\Phi^{N}\in C_{b}^{2}(O_{N})
, with
\Phi^{N}({x})=\infty
for
{x}\notin\overline{O_{N}}
.
Suppose
\Phi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi^{N}({x})=\Phi({x})\quad\text{ in $C_{b}^{2}(S_{R})$ for each $R\in\mathbb{N}$},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi({x})\geq 0\quad\text{ for all }{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.13)

ii
Let
\Psi^{N}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
satisfy

\displaystyle\Psi^{N}({x},{y})=0\text{ for }({x},{y})\notin\overline{O_{N}\times O_{N}},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\Psi^{N}({x},{y})=\Psi({x}-{y})\text{ in $C_{b}^{2}(S_{Q}^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon}^{2d})$ for each $Q\in\mathbb{N},\,\epsilon>0$},

(2.15)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{x}

\displaystyle\Psi^{N}({x},{y})=c_{\small\ref{;1a}}^{N}\delta_{{y}}({x})-\psi^{N}({x},{y})\text{ for }({x},{y})\in O_{N}\times O_{N}

(2.16)

where
\delta_{{y}}
is the delta measure at
{y}
,
c_{2.1}^{N}
is a positive constant, and
\psi^{N}\in C_{b}(O_{N}\times O_{N})
satisfies, for some constant
c_{2.2}
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\psi^{N}\leq c_{\small\ref{;32!}}/N.

(2.17)

(
A2
)
i

\mu^{N}
has the labeled density
\mathfrak{m}^{N}
with respect to
d\mathbf{{x}}^{N}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}d{x}^{i}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{N}(\mathbf{{x}}^{N})=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}^{N}}\exp\Big(-\beta\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{N}({x}^{i})+\sum_{j,k=1,\,j\neq k}^{N}\Psi^{N}({x}^{j},{x}^{k})\Big\}\Big),

(2.18)

where
0<\beta<\infty
and
\mathscr{Z}^{N}
is the normalizing constant:

\displaystyle\mathscr{Z}^{N}=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}}\exp\Big(-\beta\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi^{N}({x}^{i})+\sum_{j,k=1,\,j\neq k}^{N}\Psi^{N}({x}^{j},{x}^{k})\Big\}\Big)d\mathbf{{x}}^{N}.

(2.19)

ii
There exists a subsequence
\{\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\}
converging weakly in
\mathsf{S}
to some random point field
\mu
:

\displaystyle\mu=\lim_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\quad\text{ weakly in }\mathsf{S}

(2.20)

such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\int_{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{s}(S_{Q})\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}(d\mathsf{s})<\infty\quad\text{ for each }Q\in\mathbb{N},

(2.21)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{s}(S_{R})\mu(d\mathsf{s})=O(R^{\ell_{0}})\quad\text{ as }R\to\infty\text{ for some $\ell_{0}>0$},

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mu(\{\mathsf{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\infty\})=1.

(2.23)

Remark 2.3
.

i
From (
2.21
),
\{\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\}_{\mathfrak{n}\in N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
is tight, and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{s}(S_{Q})\mu(d\mathsf{s})<\infty\quad\text{ for each }Q\in\mathbb{N}.

ii
Condition (
2.22
) requires that the expected number of particles in
S_{R}
grows at most polynomially. This ensures that the logarithmic derivative of
\mu
can be constructed. Assumption (
2.23
) simply excludes the trivial finite-particle case.

iii
We do not assume a priori that
\mu
admits a density or correlation functions; these follow from our assumptions (see Theorem
4.1
).

iv The conditions
\Delta\Phi\geq 0
and
\psi^{N}\geq 0
can be relaxed if the constant
c_{\small\ref{;22b}}(N)+4\beta c_{\small\ref{;22c}}(N)
in (
4.6
) is non-negative for sufficient large
N
.

Let
\overline{S}_{R}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{d};\lvert s\rvert\leq R\}
and

S_{R+\epsilon}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{d};\lvert s\rvert<R+\epsilon\}
.

For a function
f
, we set
f^{(\mathbf{i})}=\partial^{k}f/\partial{x}^{\mathbf{i}}
for
\mathbf{i}\in\mathbf{I}(k)
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}(k)=\{\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in(\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N})^{d}\,;\,i_{1}+\cdots+i_{d}=k\}

(2.24)

and
{x}^{\mathbf{i}}=x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{i_{d}}
for
x=(x_{1}\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mathbb{I}(l)=\sqcup_{k=0}^{l}\mathbf{I}(k)
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{R}(l)=\{{x}\in S_{R};\det[\,{x}_{\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{i}}\,]_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{I}(l)}\not=0\},\quad{x}_{\mathbf{j}}^{\mathbf{i}}=({x}_{1}^{j_{1}})^{i_{1}}\cdots({x}_{d}^{j_{d}})^{i_{d}}.

(2.25)

(
A3
)
For
\ell_{0}
in (
2.22
), there exists an
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\ell>\ell_{0}-d
and the following:

i
For each
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\Psi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}({x},y)=\Psi({x}-{y})\quad\text{ in $C_{b}^{\ell+2}(\overline{S}_{R}^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon}^{2d})$}.

(2.26)

ii We set
\mathsf{y}=\sum_{i}\delta_{{y}^{i}}
.
For each
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbf{I}(\ell+1)}\int_{\mathsf{S}}\sum_{{y}^{i}\notin S_{R+\epsilon}}^{\infty}\sup_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\big\lVert(-\nabla_{{x}}\Psi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}})^{(\mathbf{i})}({x},{y}^{i})\big\rVert_{C^{1}(\overline{S}_{R})}d\mu(\mathsf{y})<\infty.

(2.27)

Here, we set
(-\nabla_{{x}}\Psi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}})^{(\mathbf{i})}({x},{y})=0
for
{y}\notin O_{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
although
\Psi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}({x},{y})
may be discontinuous in
{y}
on
\partial O_{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
.

iii
For
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}=\sum_{i}\delta_{{y}^{i}}
and each
R\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\sum_{{y}^{i}\not\in\overline{S}_{R}}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\nabla_{{x}}\Psi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}({x},{y}^{i})-\nabla\Psi({x}-{y}^{i})=0\quad\text{ for some }{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{R}(\ell),

(2.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\max_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbf{I}(\ell+1)}\sum_{{y}^{i}\not\in\overline{S}_{R}}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\Big\lVert(\nabla_{{x}}\Psi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}})^{(\mathbf{i})}({x},{y}^{i})-(\nabla\Psi)^{(\mathbf{i})}({x}-{y}^{i})\Big\rVert_{C^{1}(\overline{S}_{R})}=0.

(2.29)

Remark 2.4
.

When
\Psi^{N}=\Psi
,
(
A3
)
automatically holds. Indeed, i and iii are obvious and ii follows from (
1.1
), (
2.22
), and
\ell>\ell_{0}-d
(see Lemma
5.6
).

These three assumptions are sufficient to obtain an explicit formula for the coefficient of the ISDE arising from
\mu
and to construct the stochastic dynamics as the unique, strong solution of the ISDE.

To obtain the ISDE in translation-invariant form, we further assume the following.

(
A4
)

The following convergence holds in
L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}(\mu^{[1]})
, for
\mu^{[1]}
-a.e.
({x},\mathsf{s})
, or in
C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q,\mathsf{s},\epsilon})
for each
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
and
\epsilon>0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{i}\Big(1_{S_{R}}(s^{i})-1_{S_{R}}({x}-s^{i})\Big)\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})=\nabla\Phi(x).

(2.30)

Remark 2.5
.

The assumptions imposed on the limit random point field are mild. We believe that our mild assumptions allow for the construction of numerous examples of random point fields with Coulomb interactions to which our theorems can be applied.

2.3.
Explicit formulae of logarithmic derivatives: Theorem
2.1

Let
\mathbf{I}(\ell+1)
be as in
(
A3
)
.
We define the function
\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}\in C^{1}(\overline{S}_{R})
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}({x})=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbf{I}(\ell+1)}\frac{{x}^{\mathbf{i}}}{i_{1}!\cdots i_{d}!}\Big(\sum_{s^{i}\not\in\overline{S}_{R}}^{\infty}(-\nabla\Psi)^{(\mathbf{i})}(\xi_{{x}}-s^{i})\Big),

(2.31)

where

\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
and
\xi_{{x}}
is determined via the Taylor expansion of
-\nabla\Psi({x}-{y})
at
{x}=0
.
In (
2.31
), we take
\xi_{{x}}\in C^{1}(\overline{S}_{R})
and
\lvert\xi_{{x}}\rvert<\lvert{x}\rvert\leq R
.
From (
5.19
), the infinite sum over
i
in (
2.31
) converges absolutely in
C^{1}(\overline{S}_{R})
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
(see Proposition
5.1
).

Let
\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}
be the constant given by (
5.35
).
Then,
\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}
and
\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}
are independent of
\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}}(\mathsf{s})
such that

\displaystyle\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}=\eta_{R,\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s})}^{\mathbf{i}},\quad\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}=\mathscr{R}_{R,\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s})}^{\ell},

(2.32)

where the maps
\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}},\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}^{c}}:\mathsf{S}\to\mathsf{S}
are defined by

\displaystyle\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}}(\mathsf{s})=\mathsf{s}(\cdot\cap\overline{S}_{R}),\qquad\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s})=\mathsf{s}(\cdot\cap\overline{S}_{R}^{c}).

In Theorem
4.1
, we establish the existence of the one-point correlation function of
\mu
.
Accordingly, the one-reduced Campbell measure is defined by

\displaystyle\mu^{[1]}(A\times B)=\int_{A}\rho^{1}(x)\,\mu_{x}(B)\,dx,

where
\mu_{x}=\mu(\cdot+\delta_{x}\mid\mathsf{s}(\{x\})\geq 1)
denotes the reduced Palm measure of
\mu
conditioned at
x
.

Theorem 2.1

(Explicit formulae of logarithmic derivatives)
.

Let
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
.

i

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then,
\mu
possesses a
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}(\mu^{[1]})

defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})=\beta\Big(-\nabla\Phi({x})+\sum_{s^{i}\in\overline{S}_{R}}\frac{{x}-s^{i}}{\lvert{x}-s^{i}\rvert^{d}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{I}(\ell)}\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}{x}^{\mathbf{i}}+\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}({x})\Big)

(2.33)

for each
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu^{[1]}
-a.e.
({x},\mathsf{s})\in S_{R}\times\mathsf{S}
, where we set
\mathbb{I}(\ell)=\sqcup_{k=0}^{\ell}\mathbf{I}(k)
.
There exists a
\mu^{[1]}
-version of
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})
that is locally Lipschitz continuous in
x
on

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\neq}(\mathsf{s}):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x\neq s^{i}\}.

ii

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
and each
Q\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}=0\text{ for all }\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{I}(\ell),\quad\lim_{R\to\infty}\big\lVert\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}\big\rVert_{C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q})}=0,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})=\beta\Big(-\nabla\Phi({x})+\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{s^{i}\in\overline{S}_{R}}\frac{{x}-s^{i}}{\lvert{x}-s^{i}\rvert^{d}}\Big)\quad\text{ in $C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q,\mathsf{s},\epsilon})$}

(2.35)

for each
\epsilon>0
,
where
\overline{S}_{Q,\mathsf{s},\epsilon}=\{{x}\in\overline{S}_{Q};\lvert{x}-s^{i}\rvert\geq\epsilon\text{ for all }i\}
.

iii

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A4
)
. Then, the
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}
of
\mu
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})=\lim_{R\to\infty}\beta\sum_{|{x}-s^{i}|\leq R}\frac{{x}-s^{i}}{|{x}-s^{i}\rvert^{d}}.

(2.36)

The convergence in (
2.36
) is understood in the sense specified by assumption
(
A4
)
. More precisely, it may hold in one of the following ways: (i) in
L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}(\mu^{[1]})
; (ii)
\mu^{[1]}
-almost everywhere; or (iii) for each
\epsilon>0
and each
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, in
C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q,\mathsf{s},\epsilon})
for
\mu
-almost every configuration
\mathsf{s}
, in accordance with (
2.30
).

Remark 2.6
.

Clearly, (
2.34
) implies that, for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\beta\Big(\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{I}(\ell)}\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}{x}^{\mathbf{i}}+\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}({x})\Big)=0\quad\text{ in $C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q})$}.

(2.37)

The existence of the logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}
implies a natural
\mu
-reversible diffusion process
[
37
]
.
Using
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}
, the ISDE describing the
\mathsf{S}
-labeled dynamics corresponding to the
\mathsf{S}
-valued diffusion process
[
25
]
can be written as

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=B_{t}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{d}^{\mu}\Big(X_{u}^{i},\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{X_{u}^{j}}\Big)du,\quad i\in\mathbb{N},

(2.38)

where
B_{t}^{i}
are independent standard Brownian motions.

From (
2.35
), the ISDE (
2.38
) can be written for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
as

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=B_{t}^{i}-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\nabla\Phi(X_{u}^{i})du+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{|X_{u}^{j}|\leq R,\,j\neq i}\frac{X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}}{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{d}}du.

If
(
A4
)
holds in addition, then, using (
2.36
), the ISDE (
2.38
) can be written as

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=B_{t}^{i}+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}|\leq R,\,j\neq i}\frac{X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}}{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{d}}du\quad(i\in\mathbb{N}).

The drift coefficient
(1/2)\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})
is not defined on the full configuration space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}
. Consequently, a central difficulty in the analysis of the ISDEs for interacting Brownian motions is to ensure that the particle dynamics
\mathbf{X}
almost surely avoid the subset where the coefficient is undefined. Moreover, since the gradient of the Coulomb potential
\Psi
, denoted by
\nabla\Psi
, fails to be integrable at infinity, this introduces an additional technical challenge in establishing the existence and uniqueness of solutions. These considerations play a crucial role in rigorously constructing the associated dynamics and in formulating the ISDE in a mathematically precise manner.

2.4.
Existence of weak solutions: Theorem
2.2

Let
\mathfrak{a}\!:\!\mathbb{R}^{d}\!\to\!(\mathbb{R})^{d^{2}}
, where
\mathfrak{a}=(\mathfrak{a}_{kl})_{k,l=1}^{d}
, be a uniformly elliptic, bounded, symmetric matrix-valued function such that
\mathfrak{a}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Assume that there exists a constant
c_{2.3}\geq 1
satisfying

\displaystyle c_{\small\ref{;12}}^{-1}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}\leq\sum_{k,l=1}^{d}\mathfrak{a}_{kl}\xi_{k}\xi_{l}\leq c_{\small\ref{;12}}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2},\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.39)

Let
\sigma=(\sigma_{k,l})_{k,l=1}^{d}\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a matrix such that
\sigma^{t}\sigma=\mathfrak{a}
. Define

\displaystyle\nabla\mathfrak{a}=\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{a}_{kl}}{\partial x_{k}}\Big)_{l=1}^{d},\quad\mathfrak{b}=\frac{1}{2}\{\nabla\mathfrak{a}+\mathsf{d}^{\mu}\mathfrak{a}\}.

(2.40)

We generalize ISDE (
2.38
) to an elliptic-type ISDE such that

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{u}^{i})dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{i},\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{X_{u}^{j}})du\quad(i\in\mathbb{N}).

(2.41)

Here,
B^{i}=(B^{i,1},\ldots,B^{i,d})
and

\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{u}^{i})dB_{u}^{i}=(\sum_{l=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{k,l}(X_{u}^{i})dB_{u}^{i,l})_{k=1}^{d}
.

Loosely speaking, a pair
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{B})
of an
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued continuous stochastic process
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and an
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued
\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}
-Brownian motion
\mathbf{B}=(B^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, defined on a filtered space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\},P)
, is called a weak solution of (
2.41
)
if (
2.41
) holds for
P
-a.s.

A weak solution
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{B})
is called a strong solution if, in addition,
\mathbf{X}
is measurable with respect to
\mathbf{B}
. See Subsection
13.4
or
[
34
,
16
]
for the precise definitions.

Furthermore, Subsection
13.4
and
[
34
,
16
]
also discuss the notions of uniqueness of weak solutions, uniqueness of strong solutions, and pathwise uniqueness.

For
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, the associated
m
-labeled process
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{[m]}_{t}=(X_{t}^{1},\ldots,X_{t}^{m},\sum_{j>m}\delta_{X_{t}^{j}}).

(2.42)

We set
\mathbf{X}_{t}^{[0]}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}
and
\mu^{[0]}=\mu
.
We also write
\mathsf{X}_{t}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}\!:\!(\sqcup_{k=0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k})\sqcup(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}\!\to\!\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the map defined by

\mathfrak{u}((x^{i}))=\sum_{i}\delta_{{x}^{i}}
, where
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of all measures on
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. If
k=0
, we interpret
\mathfrak{u}(x)=\mathsf{0}
for
{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{0}
by convention, where
\mathsf{0}
denotes the zero measure.

A label
\mathfrak{l}\!:\!\mathsf{S}\backslash\{\mathsf{0}\}\!\to\!(\sqcup_{k=1}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k})\sqcup(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
is a measurable map such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}\circ\mathfrak{l}=\mathrm{id.}.

We write
\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})=(\mathfrak{l}^{i}(\mathsf{s}))_{i}
.
Throughout this paper, we fix a label such that the absolute values of particles are non-decreasing:

\displaystyle\text{$|\mathfrak{l}^{i}(\mathsf{s})|\leq|\mathfrak{l}^{i+1}(\mathsf{s})|$ for all $i$ and
$\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}\backslash\{\mathsf{0}\}$}.

(2.43)

From (
2.23
),
\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
.
For
\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}
, we often write
\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})=(s^{i})_{i}
.

Let
a=\{a(R)\}_{R\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence of natural numbers. Let

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}[a]=\bigcap_{R=1}^{\infty}\{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}\,;\,\mathsf{s}(S_{R})\leq a(R)\}.

(2.44)

Then,
\mathsf{K}[a]
is a compact set in
\mathsf{S}
. Indeed, a subset
{A}\subset\mathsf{S}
is relatively compact if and only if
{A}\subset\mathsf{K}[a]
for some
a
(see
[
3
,
12
]
).
Because
\{\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\}_{\mathsf{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
\mu
,
\{\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}\}_{\mathsf{n}\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.
Hence, for each
\mathsf{r}\in\mathbb{N}
, we can take
a_{\mathsf{r}}=\{a_{\mathsf{r}}(R)\}_{R\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}(\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{r}}])>1-\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}}.

(2.45)

From (
2.45
) and the weak convergence of
\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
to
\mu
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{r}}])>1-\frac{1}{\mathsf{r}}.

We note that
\{\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{r}}]\}_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathbb{N}}
becomes an increasing sequence of compact sets in
\mathsf{S}
.

Let
\mu^{[m]}
be the
m
-reduced Campbell measure of
\mu
(cf.
[
3
]
).
Suppose that the
m
-point correlation function
\rho^{m}
of
\mu
exists. Then, we define
\mu^{[m]}
by

\displaystyle\mu^{[m]}=\rho^{m}(\mathbf{x})\mu_{\mathbf{x}}(d\mathsf{s})d\mathbf{x},

(2.46)

where
\mu_{\mathbf{x}}=\mu(\cdot+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{{x}^{i}}|\mathsf{s}(\{{x}^{i}\})\geq 1,1\leq i\leq m)
is the
m
-reduced Palm measure conditioned at
\mathbf{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{m})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}
.

Even if
\mu
does not admit an
m
-point correlation function, we can still define the
m
-reduced Campbell measure by localization.
Let
\mu_{\mathsf{r}}=\mu(\cdot\cap K[\mathsf{r}])
.
Suppose that
\mu
has density functions.
Then, from (
2.44
),
\mu_{\mathsf{r}}
has the
m
-point correlation function
\rho_{\mathsf{r}}^{m}
and the associated
m
-reduced Campbell measure
\mu^{[m]}_{\mathsf{r}}
similarly as (
2.46
).
Hence, we define
\mu^{[m]}
as the increasing limit of
\mu^{[m]}_{\mathsf{r}}
:

\displaystyle\mu^{[m]}(A)=\lim_{\mathsf{r}\to\infty}\mu^{[m]}_{\mathsf{r}}(A)\quad\text{ for }A\in\mathscr{B}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}).

(2.47)

We note that
\mu^{[m]}
is independent of the particular choice of compact sets
\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{r}}]
.
In Theorem
4.1
, we will prove that
\mu
has a
k
-density function on
\overline{S}_{R}^{k}
for any
R,k\in\mathbb{N}
and one-point correlation function.

Theorem 2.2

(Existence of weak solutions)
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then ISDE (
2.41
) has a weak solution
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
starting at
\mathbf{s}=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
, such that the associated
m
-labeled process
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
is a
\mu^{[m]}
-symmetric and conservative diffusion for each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

We refer to
[
5
]
for the concepts of conservative, reversible, and symmetric diffusions.
From Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2
, we immediately have the following.

Corollary 1
.

i

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
.
Then, the results in Theorem
2.2
apply to ISDE (
2.48
):

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{u}^{i})dB_{u}^{i}+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\nabla\mathfrak{a}(X_{u}^{i})du

(2.48)

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(-\nabla\Phi(X_{u}^{i})+\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{\lvert X_{u}^{j}\rvert\leq R,\,j\neq i}\frac{X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}}{|X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{d}}\Big)\mathfrak{a}(X_{u}^{i})du\quad(i\in\mathbb{N}).

ii

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A4
)
.
Then, the results in Theorem
2.2
apply to ISDE (
2.49
):

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{u}^{i})dB_{u}^{i}+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\nabla\mathfrak{a}(X_{u}^{i})du

(2.49)

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert\leq R,\,j\neq i}\frac{X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}}{|X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{d}}\Big)\mathfrak{a}(X_{u}^{i})du\quad(i\in\mathbb{N}).

2.5.
Unique strong solutions: Theorem
2.3

We next turn to the construction of unique strong solutions to ISDE (
2.41
).
In Theorem
2.3
, we establish the existence of such strong solutions and prove their pathwise uniqueness under suitable conditions. To this end, we introduce the notion of the tail decomposition of
\mu
.

Let
\mathscr{T}(\mathsf{S})=\cap_{R=1}^{\infty}\sigma[\pi_{S_{R}^{c}}]
be the tail
\sigma
-field.
Let
\mu_{\mathsf{a}}=\mu(\,\cdot\,|\mathscr{T}(\mathsf{S}))(\mathsf{a})
be the regular conditional probability of
\mu
given
\mathscr{T}(\mathsf{S})
. Then,

\displaystyle\mu=\int\mu_{\mathsf{a}}\mu(d\mathsf{a}).

Definition 2.3

(Tail decomposition of
\mu
)
.

A random point field
\mu
is said to have a tail decomposition if there exists an
\mathsf{S}_{0}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{S})
such that
\mu(\mathsf{S}_{0})=1
and, for all
\mathsf{a}\in\mathsf{S}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{a}}(\mathsf{A})\in\{0,1\}\text{ for all }\mathsf{A}\in\mathscr{T}(\mathsf{S}),

(2.50)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathsf{a}}([\mathsf{a}])=1,\quad\text{ where $[\mathsf{a}]=\{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{S}_{0};\mu_{\mathsf{a}}=\mu_{\mathsf{b}}\}$},

(2.51)

\displaystyle\text{$\mu_{\mathsf{a}}$ and $\mu_{\mathsf{b}}$ are mutually singular on $\mathscr{T}(\mathsf{S})$ if $\mu_{\mathsf{a}}\not=\mu_{\mathsf{b}}$}.

(2.52)

This decomposition provides the framework for discussing the construction of strong solutions and the proof of pathwise uniqueness.

Remark 2.7
.

i
Let
\mathsf{S}_{0}
be the set in Definition
2.3
.
Let
\mathsf{a}\in\mathsf{S}_{0}
.
Let
\mathsf{A}\in\mathscr{T}(\mathsf{S})
such that
\mathsf{a}\in\mathsf{A}
. Then,
\mu_{\mathsf{a}}(\mathsf{A})=1
from (
2.51
).

ii
Similar to
[
34
, Lem. 14.2]
, we can prove that
\mu
has a tail decomposition.

Let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}}=\{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}\,;\,\mathsf{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\infty,\mathsf{s}(\{s\})\in\{0,1\}\text{ for all }s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.
Let
W(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})
be the set of all
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}}
-valued continuous paths defined on
[0,\infty)
.
We set

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})=\{\mathsf{w}\in W(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}});\mathsf{w}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\delta_{w_{t}^{i}},\mathbf{w}=(w^{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}\}.

(2.53)

For
\mathbf{w}=(w^{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
, we set

\displaystyle I_{R,T}(\mathbf{w})=\sup\{i\in\mathbb{N};\min_{t\in[0,T]}\lvert w^{i}(t)\rvert\leq R\}.

(2.54)

For a
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued process
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathsf{X}
be the associated unlabeled process such that
\mathsf{X}_{t}=\sum_{i}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}
.
Note that, if
\mathsf{X}\in W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})
, then
I_{R,T}(\mathbf{X})
is well defined.

We make the following assumptions. Let
\nu
be a random point field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(
SIN
)

P(\mathsf{X}\in W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}}))=1
.

(
NBJ
)

P(I_{R,T}(\mathbf{X})<\infty)=1
for each
R,T\in\mathbb{N}
.

(
AC
)
ν

P\circ\mathsf{X}_{t}^{-1}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu
for all
0<t<\infty
.

Intuitively, these assumptions mean the following. Condition
(
SIN
)
ensures that the particle system almost surely remains in the well-behaved state space
W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})
. Condition
(
NBJ
)
guarantees that within any finite time interval
[0,T]
, only finitely many particles enter any given finite region
[-R,R]^{d}
, thus preventing an infinite influx of particles or jumps. Condition
(
AC
)
ν
ensures that the distribution of particles at each time
t
admits a density with respect to the reference measure
\nu
, making the distribution comparable to
\nu
.

In addition, we require two further conditions,
(
MF
)
and
(
IFC
)
, which are described in Subsection
13.5
and Subsection
13.4
, respectively. For the precise definitions of a strong solution, a unique strong solution, and pathwise uniqueness of strong solutions, see Definitions
13.11
–
13.13
.

Theorem 2.3

(unique strong solutions)
.

Assume
(

)
–
(

)
. Then, for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{a}
, (
2.41
) admits a unique strong solution
\mathbf{X}
starting at
\mathbf{s}=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})
for

\mathsf{s}
under constraints

)
,
(

)
,
(

)
{}_{\mu_{\mathsf{a}}}
,
(

)

(

)
. Furthermore, for each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, the associated
m

is a
\mu_{\mathsf{a}}^{[m]}
-symmetric and conservative diffusion.

It is well known that the pathwise uniqueness of strong solutions ensures the uniqueness in law of weak solutions (see

).
For the concepts of uniqueness in law and pathwise uniqueness of weak solutions, see Definition

.

.

2.6.
Diffusion processes in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
: Theorem
2.4

The analysis of fully labeled dynamics plays a crucial role in understanding the structure of infinite-particle systems.
While the
m
-labeled dynamics
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
provide a useful finite-dimensional projection that admits an invariant distribution, they capture only a partial view of the system.
To obtain a complete picture, one must go beyond these finite labels and establish the existence of a genuine diffusion on the full label space
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
.
This step is indispensable, since it ensures that the dynamics are well-defined not merely in restricted subsystems but for the entire infinite system.

Historically, the construction of fully labeled dynamics has been regarded as a highly challenging problem.
Even in the context of determinantal processes such as the Dyson model, establishing such dynamics required substantial effort, with delicate arguments based on random matrix theory.
For general Coulomb systems, the situation is even more severe: the long-range nature of the interaction and the absence of classical Gibbsian structures prevent the direct application of Dirichlet form methods or invariant measure techniques.
Against this backdrop, proving the existence of fully labeled conservative diffusions represents a significant advance, as it overcomes obstacles that have persisted for decades.

In Theorem
2.4
, we prove that the fully labeled process
\mathbf{X}
is indeed an
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued conservative diffusion.
Unlike
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
, however, the process
\mathbf{X}
possesses no invariant measure, reflecting a fundamental difference between finite-label and full-label dynamics.
Nevertheless, the result guarantees that the fully labeled dynamics form a legitimate stochastic process with infinite lifetime, thereby providing a rigorous foundation for the dynamical theory of Coulomb systems.

As before, for
\mathbf{s}=(s^{i})_{i}
we write
\mathfrak{u}(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
.
Let
\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
denote the subset defined in (
11.13
).

Theorem 2.4

(Diffusion processes in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
.
Let
\mathbf{X}
be the solution of the ISDE (
2.41
) constructed in Theorem
2.3
.
Then there exists a measurable subset
\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\mathbf{X}
is a conservative diffusion with state space
\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}
and

\mu(\mathfrak{u}(\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}))=1
.

Here, saying that
\mathbf{X}
is a conservative diffusion with state space
\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}
means that
the regular conditional law

P_{\mathbf{x}}:=P\big(\mathbf{X}\in\cdot\,\big|\,\mathbf{X}_{0}=\mathbf{x}\big)

admits a version such that
\{P_{\mathbf{x}}\}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}}
forms a family of probability measures on

C([0,\infty);\mathbf{S}_{\star\star})
with the strong Markov property and infinite lifetime
[
5
]
.
In particular, the diffusion law
P_{\mathbf{x}}
is defined for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}
.

Remark 2.9
.

i
The state space can in principle be extended to the whole
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
by freezing the dynamics outside
\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}
.
The set
\mathbf{S}_{\star\star}
is given explicitly by (
11.13
).

ii
It is plausible that the diffusion admits no invariant probability measure.
An analogous statement is known for the Dyson model
[
36
]
.
In particular, this suggests that there may be no Dirichlet form naturally associated with the diffusion.

2.7.
Finite-volume approximation: Theorem
2.5

Next, we consider a finite-particle approximation of dynamics.
We impose reflecting boundary conditions on
\partial S_{R}
and study the dynamics of the particle systems
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N}
and
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
, in which particles outside the region
\overline{S}_{R}
are held fixed.

Let
\mathsf{d}^{N}
be the logarithmic derivative of
\mu^{N}
. We write
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\delta_{s^{i}}
.
From (
2.18
),

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{N}({x},\mathsf{s})=-\beta\Big(\nabla\Phi^{N}({x})+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\nabla_{{x}}\Psi^{N}({x},s^{i})\Big).

Let
\mathfrak{b}^{N}=\{\nabla\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{a}\mathsf{d}^{N}\}
.
Then, the SDE describing the
N
-particle dynamics is such that, for
1\leq i\leq N
,

\displaystyle X_{t}^{N,i}-X_{0}^{N,i}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{u}^{N,i})dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}^{N}(X_{u}^{N,i},\sum_{j\neq i}^{N}\delta_{X_{u}^{N,j}})du.

(2.55)

We impose reflecting boundary conditions on
\partial S_{R}
in SDE (
2.55
) and hold the particles outside the region
\overline{S}_{R}=\{\lvert s\rvert\leq R\}
fixed. Under these conditions, the SDE becomes

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}^{N}(\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(u),\sum_{j\neq i}^{N}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{N,j}(u)})du

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(u))dL_{R}^{N,i}(u),

\displaystyle L_{R}^{N,i}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\partial S_{R}}(\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(u))dL_{R}^{N,i}(u),\quad\text{for }i\leq\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R}),

(2.56)

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{N,i}(0)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{for }\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R})<i\leq N.

(2.57)

Here,
\mathbf{n}_{R}
denotes the inward normal vector on the boundary
\partial S_{R}
, and
L_{R}^{N,i}
is a non-negative, non-decreasing continuous process referred to as the local time on the boundary
\partial\overline{S}_{R}
(cf.
[
7
, pp. 251–256]
).

We set the initial condition
\mathbf{X}_{R}^{N}(0)=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})
for
\mu^{N}
-almost every
\mathsf{s}
, where
\mathfrak{l}=(\mathfrak{l}^{i})_{i}
is the labeling introduced in (
2.43
).
From (
2.43
) and (
2.57
), all particles outside the region
\overline{S}_{R}
are held fixed.

Let
\{N_{\mathfrak{n}}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be the sequence in
(
A2
)
such that
\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
converges to
\mu
.
Taking the limit
\mathfrak{n}\to\infty
in (
2.56
)–(
2.57
), we obtain the ISDE

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{j}(u)})du

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u),

\displaystyle L_{R}^{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}1_{\partial S_{R}}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u),\quad\text{for }i\leq\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R}),

(2.58)

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{for }\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R})<i<\infty.

(2.59)

Remark 2.10
.

i
For
\mathbf{s}=(s^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
such that
s^{i}\neq s^{j}
for any
i\neq j
,
SDE (
2.56
)–(
2.57
) with
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N}(0)=\mathbf{s}
has a unique strong solution
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N}
.
Indeed, the coefficients are smooth outside
\mathscr{N}=\cup_{i\neq j}^{N}\{s^{i}=s^{j}\}
and
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N}
does not hit
\mathscr{N}
because
d\geq 2
. See Theorem
6.1
.

ii The same as above holds for SDE (
2.58
)–(
2.59
) for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
.
This claim follows from (
2.33
), (
2.39
), (
2.40
), and
\mathfrak{a}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Hence, we denote by
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
the unique strong solution for SDE (
2.58
)–(
2.59
).

We equip
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the topology of uniform convergence on each bounded interval and endow
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
with the corresponding product topology. With this topology,
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
becomes a Polish space.

Let
\mathfrak{l}_{N}=(\mathfrak{l}^{1},\dots,\mathfrak{l}^{N})
, where
\mathfrak{l}=(\mathfrak{l}^{i})_{i}
is the labeling introduced in (
2.43
). Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathfrak{l}_{N}(\mathsf{s})=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})\quad\text{ for $\mu$-a.s.\,$\mathsf{s}$}.

Let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
be a solution of (
2.56
)–(
2.57
), and let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
and
\mathbf{X}
be solutions of (
2.58
)–(
2.59
) and (
2.41
), respectively, as described in Remark
2.10
and Theorem
2.3
.
We impose

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}(0)=\mathfrak{l}_{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}(\mathsf{s}),\quad\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}(0)=\mathbf{X}(0)=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s}).

(2.60)

Therefore, using the label
\mathfrak{l}
, we couple the initial staring points of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
,
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
, and
\mathbf{X}
.

Let
c_{2.4}
be a positive constant. Let

\displaystyle\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathsf{S}),\quad 0\leq\varphi\leq c_{\small\ref{;14}},\quad\int_{\mathsf{S}}\varphi d\mu=1.

(2.61)

Let
\varphi d\mu
be the random point field such that
(\varphi d\mu)(A)=\int_{A}\varphi d\mu
.

Theorem 2.5

(Finite-volume approximation)
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
.
Let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
be the solution of (
2.56
)–(
2.57
) with initial condition

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}(\varphi d\mu)\circ(\mathfrak{l}_{N_{\mathfrak{n}}})^{-1}.

(2.62)

Let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
and
\mathbf{X}
be the solutions of (
2.58
)–(
2.59
) and (
2.41
) as in Remark
2.10
and Theorem
2.3
with initial condition

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}(0)=\mathbf{X}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}(\varphi d\mu)\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}.

(2.63)

Then, we have the following convergence in distribution in
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}\quad\text{ in distribution in }C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}},

(2.64)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}\quad\text{ in distribution in }C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}.

(2.65)

Here, we regard
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
as
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued process by taking
\underline{X}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}},i}(t)=\underline{X}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}},i}(0)=\mathfrak{l}^{i}(\mathsf{s})
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
and
i>N_{\mathfrak{n}}
, where
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}=(\underline{X}_{R}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}},i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

2.8.
Related works and positioning

Hyperuniformity

We have computed the logarithmic derivatives of random point fields with two-dimensional Coulomb interaction potentials for the sine
β
, Airy
β

(\beta=1,2,4)
, Bessel
2
, and Ginibre random point fields
[
25
,
26
,
35
,
10
]
.
These computations require strong hyperuniformity of particle numbers within
S_{R}
for each
R\in\mathbb{N}
, a property that must be established separately for each random point field.
For example, in the case of the Ginibre random point field, we used the following:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Var}^{\mu^{N}}[N_{R}(\mathsf{s})]=O(R)\quad\text{ as }R\to\infty.

(2.66)

Here,
N_{R}(\mathsf{s})
is the number of particles in
S_{R}

[
39
,
26
,
31
,
32
]
.
Such an estimate is called hyperuniform because, if
\mu^{N}
is replaced by
\Lambda^{N}
, then the right-hand side becomes
O(R^{2})

[
20
]
.
Here,
\Lambda^{N}
is the conditioned Poisson random point field with Lebesgue intensity
dx
defined as

\displaystyle\Lambda^{N}=\Lambda(\cdot|\mathsf{s}(S_{R(N)})=N,\mathsf{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash S_{R(N)})=0),

where

R(N)
is the positive number such that the volume of
S_{R(N)}
coincides with
N
.
We also note that
\Lambda^{N}
converges to
\Lambda
as
N\to\infty
.

We set
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
. From (
2.66
), we have that, for each
Q\in\mathbb{N}
and
{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\int_{S_{Q}\times\mathsf{S}}\Big\lvert\Big(\sum_{\lvert{x}-s^{i}\rvert\geq R}\frac{{x}-s^{i}}{\lvert{x}-s^{i}\rvert^{2}}\Big)-{x}\Big\rvert^{2}d\mu^{N,[1]}=0.

(2.67)

The corrector
{x}
appears in the summand in (
2.67
) because the measure
\mu^{N}
lacks translation invariance and the gradient of the Coulomb potential is not integrable at infinity. From this expression, we obtained the logarithmic derivative of the Ginibre random point field
[
25
,
34
]
, highlighting the crucial role of hyperuniformity—similar to (
2.66
)—in the construction of logarithmic derivatives. While Leblé
[
20
]
established a hyperuniform estimate for general
\beta>0
, it is significantly weaker than (
2.66
) and insufficient for our purposes. For the sine
β
, Airy
β

(\beta=1,2,4)
, Bessel
2
, and Ginibre random point fields, uniform estimates for the associated
N
-particle systems were obtained in
[
25
,
26
,
35
,
10
]
, relying on the specific structure of each field and techniques from random matrix theory and determinantal random point fields. However, these proofs do not extend to general Coulomb random point fields, except in the Ginibre case, necessitating a new approach.

Quenching

In this work we introduce a robust method for estimating the logarithmic derivatives of
\mu^{N}
(Lemma
4.3
) and refine it for conditional probabilities
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{N}
(Lemma
5.2
), exploiting a form of harmonicity in the interaction potential. As a central result, we obtain the uniform bound

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{S_{Q}\times\mathsf{S}}\bigl|\mathsf{d}^{N}({x},\mathsf{s})\bigr|^{2}\,\mu^{N,[1]}(d{x}d\mathsf{s})<\infty,

(2.68)

which provides the necessary control to rigorously define logarithmic derivatives in the Coulomb setting.

The proof of (
2.68
) is remarkably simple: it relies only on (
2.16
), (
2.17
), and the basic definition of the logarithmic derivative.
The estimate itself may be viewed as a form of hyperuniformity, since the quantity in (
2.68
) diverges when
\mu^{N,[1]}
is replaced by the one-Campbell measure
\Lambda^{N,[1]}
of the conditioned Poisson point process
\Lambda^{N}
, even after excluding a neighborhood of
{x}
.

In Lemma
5.2
, we establish the
quenched analogue
of (
2.68
):

\displaystyle\sup_{R\geq Q+1}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{S_{Q}\times\mathsf{S}}\bigl\lvert\mathsf{d}_{R,\mathsf{y}^{N}}^{N}({x},\mathsf{s})\bigr\rvert^{2}\,\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}^{N}}^{N,[1]}(d{x}d\mathsf{s})<\infty\quad\text{ for $\mu$-a.s., $\mathsf{y}$},

(2.69)

where
\mathsf{d}_{R,\mathsf{y}^{N}}^{N}
denotes the logarithmic derivative of
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}^{N}}^{N}
, and
\mathsf{y}^{N}
is defined in (
5.3
).

Inequality (
2.69
) plays a decisive role in the proof of Theorem
2.1
.
Indeed, it fulfills the role previously played by strong hyperuniform estimates, but with a crucial difference: its proof is
significantly simpler
.
Earlier results relied on bounding average fluctuations in the exterior configuration, whereas here we directly control the external condition in a quenched form.
This shift in perspective—replacing averaged estimates by a quenched analysis—is one of the key conceptual innovations of the present paper.

Logarithmic derivatives

The concept of the logarithmic derivative for random point fields was introduced in
[
25
]
. The present paper further develops this idea and presents the
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative (see Definition
2.2
).
Combining the concept of the
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative, the coupling of logarithmic derivatives in (
5.5
), and a quench
L^{2}
-bound of logarithmic derivatives in Lemma
5.2
and Theorem
5.1
, we derive an explicit formula for the logarithmic derivative in (
2.33
).
From (
2.33
), we construct the stochastic dynamics of infinite-particle systems and verify the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
.
Using (
2.33
), we prove that ISDE (
2.41
) has a unique strong solution (Theorem
2.3
).

Until now, the logarithmic derivatives of Coulomb random point fields have been explicitly computed only in the case of the two-dimensional Coulomb field at inverse temperature
\beta=2
, namely the Ginibre random point field. These computations relied heavily on the special algebraic and determinantal structure of that particular field and do not extend to general
d
-dimensional Coulomb random point fields. In contrast, we present here a new, remarkably simple and transparent method that yields uniform estimates for the logarithmic derivative of
\mu^{N}
(Lemma
4.3
) and for its conditional analogue (Lemma
5.2
), valid simultaneously for all spatial dimensions
d\geq 2
and all inverse temperatures
\beta>0
. The key insight is to exploit a certain harmonicity property of the interaction potential, which permits effective control over the infinite-dimensional interactions. Our approach is robust, conceptually clear, and provides a versatile tool for analyzing the stochastic dynamics of
d
-dimensional Coulomb random point fields, thereby overcoming the limitations of all previously known methods.

Interacting Brownian motions

Lang initiated the systematic study of interacting Brownian motions, which was subsequently developed by Fritz, Tanemura, and others in the case of finite-range interaction potentials
[
17
,
18
,
6
,
42
]
.
When the interaction becomes long-ranged, however, the problem of constructing solutions to ISDEs poses significant difficulties, even for potentials within Ruelle’s class—namely, those that are superstable and integrable at infinity.
Later, the first author introduced the Dirichlet form approach, which made it possible to establish the existence of weak solutions to ISDEs for a broad family of interaction potentials
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
. This framework encompasses, in particular, the Ginibre interacting Brownian motion.
Nonetheless, the stronger questions of constructing pathwise unique strong solutions and establishing their uniqueness remained open.

In
[
46
]
, Tsai succeeded in constructing pathwise unique strong solutions of ISDEs for the
\sin_{\beta}
random point fields with all
\beta\geq 1
. These processes correspond to infinite-particle systems in one dimension with logarithmic interactions, that is, with the two-dimensional Coulomb potential. In this sense, the ISDE in
[
46
]
can be regarded as involving a Riesz potential. Tsai’s proof relies on a delicate coupling mechanism that exploits special properties of the logarithmic interaction in one dimension. Such an approach, however, has no counterpart in higher dimensions with genuine Coulomb potentials.

Subsequently, in
[
34
,
16
]
, a general method for constructing pathwise unique strong solutions of ISDEs was developed. The central idea is to represent the ISDE as an infinite hierarchy of finite-dimensional SDEs evolving in a random environment. The well-posedness of this hierarchy is then reduced to a problem of tail triviality for the equilibrium measures of the associated unlabeled dynamics.

To apply this framework, one requires both an equilibrium state (random point field) and the corresponding logarithmic derivative. Moreover, a strong hyperuniform estimate of particle numbers in expanding balls of radius
R
is typically necessary in order to control the pronounced long-range effects of Coulomb interactions. Such sharp hyperuniform estimates, however, are highly nontrivial and are known only in special cases tied to random matrix ensembles and particular inverse temperatures.

The present work introduces a new and more robust approach to circumvent this obstacle. Rather than relying on delicate hyperuniform bounds, we develop a precise representation of the logarithmic derivative (Theorem
2.1
). This representation allows us to verify directly the key assumption—the IFC condition—required in the theory of
[
34
,
16
]
, and thereby establish the strong well-posedness of the Coulomb interacting Brownian motions for all
d\geq 2
and
\beta>0
.

Our new approach is highly robust, and its scope is not confined to Coulomb potentials.
We expect that it can be extended to Riesz potentials in arbitrary dimensions
d\geq 1
and for all inverse temperatures
\beta>0
, both in the bulk and at the soft edge.
These extensions, however, lie beyond the scope of the present work and are left for future investigation.

As already emphasized, the logarithmic derivative plays a central role in the stochastic dynamics of infinite-particle systems arising from random point fields.
There exist, however, important examples of random point fields that do not admit a logarithmic derivative in the present sense.
Indeed, in
[
37
]
, the second author proved that for random point fields exhibiting rigidities stronger than number rigidity—such as the zero set of Gaussian analytic functions
[
8
,
9
]
—a logarithmic derivative does not exist.
Developing a new framework for logarithmic derivatives suitable for such highly rigid random point fields, and establishing ISDEs for them within the scheme of
[
34
,
16
]
, thus presents a challenging and important direction for future research.

Katori and Tanemura proposed an alternative approach to constructing stochastic dynamics with logarithmic interaction in one dimension. Their method, based on space-time correlation functions, is restricted to the special case of inverse temperature
\beta=2
in one-dimensional systems. For further details, see
[
13
]
and the references therein. Subsequently, in
[
33
]
, it was shown that the dynamics obtained through this correlation function method coincide with those described by the ISDE framework developed in
[
25
,
34
,
16
]
.

Moreover,
[
33
]
investigated in detail the relationship between the logarithmic derivative and the space-time correlation approach. It was rigorously established that the stochastic dynamics constructed by these two seemingly distinct methods are, in fact, identical. This coincidence highlights the fundamental role of the logarithmic derivative in unifying different perspectives on infinite-particle dynamics with logarithmic interactions.

In
[
2
]
, Assiotis and Mirsajjadi constructed a non-equilibrium dynamics whose equilibrium distribution is the inverse Bessel random point field, and they identified the ISDE governing this dynamics.
Their method is algebraic in nature, relying on intertwining theory and the use of derivatives of the so-called “characteristic polynomial.”
This construction bears a striking resemblance to the framework of logarithmic derivatives developed in
[
25
]
.
We believe that clarifying the precise relationship between these two approaches is not merely of technical interest, but represents a fundamental step toward a unified understanding of stochastic dynamics associated with random point fields.
In particular, such a connection could open the way to extending algebraic methods to broader classes of interactions, and conversely, to interpreting analytic techniques such as logarithmic derivatives in terms of deeper algebraic structures.

In
[
40
]
, Suzuki established the ergodicity of the unlabeled dynamics of the Ginibre interacting Brownian motion, thereby providing a concrete confirmation of the general ergodicity theory (see also
[
36
,
37
]
). He further derived curvature bounds for Dyson Brownian motion in
[
41
]
, offering additional structural insight into these dynamics.

In the Ginibre case, the vanishing of self-diffusion was established in
[
28
]
, with number rigidity of the stationary field serving as the decisive mechanism. This phenomenon connects naturally with our framework, and its broader implications for translation-invariant Coulomb systems are discussed in the Introduction.
In
[
23
]
, it was proved that the self-diffusion coefficient is strictly positive for any potential in Ruelle’s class with convex hardcore.
Taken together, these results highlight the structural distinction between Coulomb interactions and Ruelle-type interactions.

Structure of the paper

After presenting our main theorems in this section, the remainder of the paper develops the analytic and probabilistic framework needed to establish them.

Section
3
presents concrete examples.
Section
4
constructs the logarithmic derivative of Coulomb random point fields, a central analytic ingredient for the ISDE theory.
Section
5
strengthens this by proving a quenched estimate and deriving an explicit representation of the logarithmic derivative.

In Section
6
, we establish non-collision properties of ISDE solutions by direct SDE techniques. These arguments do not rely on Dirichlet form theory, yet the resulting non-collision property is later used to prove uniqueness of Dirichlet forms.

Sections
7
–
9
form the technical core of the paper: we introduce Dirichlet form schemes for
m
-labeled dynamics, construct infinite-volume processes under both lower and upper Dirichlet forms.

In Section
10
, we verify the IFC condition ensuring consistency of the dynamics and upgrade the constructions to pathwise unique strong solutions of the ISDEs.

In Section
11
, we establish uniqueness of the Dirichlet forms and prove the existence of the fully labeled diffusion process in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
.

In Section
12
, we completes the program by proving convergence from finite-particle systems with reflecting boundary conditions to the infinite-particle ISDE dynamics.

Finally, the Appendix (Section
13
) gathers auxiliary material on configuration spaces and Dirichlet forms, and recalls general ISDE theory from earlier works that is essential for our arguments.

Key difficulties and techniques.

Two major obstacles underlie this program: the long-range and singular nature of Coulomb interactions. Previous methods relied on delicate hyperuniform estimates available only in special determinantal cases. Our approach replaces these with new analytic estimates for the logarithmic derivative, together with a quenched formulation that yields robust
L^{2}
bounds. This combination provides a conceptually simple yet powerful tool, allowing us to establish strong well-posedness of Coulomb interacting Brownian motions in all dimensions
d\geq 2
and for all
\beta>0
.

3.
Concrete examples

We now present concrete examples of random point fields that satisfy assumptions
(
A1
)
–
(
A4
)
.
Throughout, let
d\geq 2
and
\beta>0
, and let
\Psi
denote the
d
-dimensional Coulomb potential given in (
1.1
).

Example 3.1

(
[
1
,
44
]
)
.

Let

\Phi(x)=c_{\small\ref{;Gauss}}\,|x|^{2},

where
c_{3.1}
is a positive constant, and set

\Phi^{N}(x)=N^{2/d}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{x}{N^{1/d}}\right).

Take
\Psi^{N}=\Psi
and
O_{N}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then
(
A3
)
is automatically satisfied.

In
[
44
]
, a convergent subsequence was constructed, yielding the limiting Coulomb random point field
\mu
, which possesses bounded correlation functions of all orders together with other regularity properties.
Moreover, the assumptions in
[
44
]
are weaker than those stated above; see
[
44
, 1.25, Th. 6]
.

We now present two further specific examples of random point fields that satisfy assumptions
(
A1
)
–
(
A4
)
.
These examples exhibit a form of “invariance” under certain transformations, which follows from the invariance of the corresponding finite-particle systems.
As a consequence, the one-point correlation function is constant for each
N
-particle system.

Verifying that these examples satisfy
(
A1
)
–
(
A4
)
requires a separate argument.
The construction can also be extended to homogeneous spaces and, more generally, to compact Riemannian manifolds, including models beyond the periodic and spherical cases.
These generalizations will be presented elsewhere.

Example 3.2

(Periodic approximation)
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}]^{d}

denote the
d
-dimensional unit torus.
Set
\Phi^{N}=\Phi=0
, and let
\Psi_{\mathbb{T}}(x,y)
denote the Green’s function of

-c_{\small\ref{;per}}\,\Delta
on
\mathbb{T}
subject to periodic boundary conditions,
where
c_{3.2}
is the positive constant determined according to (
1.1
).
Equivalently,
\Psi_{\mathbb{T}}
satisfies

-c_{\small\ref{;per}}\,\Delta\Psi_{\mathbb{T}}=\delta_{0}-1\quad\text{in the sense of distributions}.

Set

O_{N}=\Bigl(-\tfrac{N}{2},\,\tfrac{N}{2}\Bigr)^{d},\qquad\Psi^{N}({x},{y})=N^{2-d}\,\Psi_{\mathbb{T}}\!\left(\frac{{x}}{N},\,\frac{{y}}{N}\right).

Note that, by construction, the measure
\mu
is invariant under translations on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Example 3.3

(Sphere approximation)
.

Let

\mathbb{S}=\{\hat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\lvert\hat{x}\rvert=1\}

denote the unit sphere, and let
\mathbf{e}_{d+1}=(0,\dots,0,1)
denote the north pole.
Define the stereographic projection

\varpi:\mathbb{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{e}_{d+1}\}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

by

\varpi(\hat{x})=\frac{\hat{x}^{\prime}}{\,1-\hat{x}_{d+1}\,},\qquad\hat{x}=(\hat{x}^{\prime},\hat{x}_{d+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R},

so that
\varpi(\hat{x})
is the intersection of the line through
\mathbf{e}_{d+1}

and
\hat{x}
with the hyperplane
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0\}
.
The inverse map
\varpi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{e}_{d+1}\}
is

\varpi^{-1}(y)=\frac{(2y,\,|y|^{2}-1)}{1+|y|^{2}},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

with Jacobian

J(y)=\frac{2^{d}}{(1+|y|^{2})^{d}}.

Set

\Phi_{\mathbb{S}}({x})=1/\lvert J(\varpi^{-1}({x}))\rvert,\qquad\Phi^{N}({x})=N^{2-d}\,\Phi_{\mathbb{S}}({x}/N),

and define

\Psi_{\mathbb{S}}\text{ as the Green's function of }-c_{\small\ref{;sp}}\,\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}\text{ on }\mathbb{S},

where
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}
is the Laplace–Beltrami operator and
c_{3.3}
is determined according to (
2.15
) and (
2.16
).
Set

\Psi^{N}({x},{y})=N^{2-d}\,\Psi_{\mathbb{S}}\!\left(\frac{{x}}{N},\,\frac{{y}}{N}\right),\qquad O_{N}=\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Note that, by construction, the measure
\mu
is invariant under the full Euclidean group
E(d)
, i.e., under all translations, rotations, and reflections.

4.
Construction of the logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}
of
\mu

In this section, we establish the existence of the logarithmic derivative of
\mu
. The section is divided into four parts.

In Subsection
4.1
, we prove the
L^{2}
-uniform bound of the logarithmic derivatives
\mathsf{d}^{N}
of the
N
-particle systems.
In Subsection
4.2
, we demonstrate the
L^{2}
-uniform bound of the logarithmic derivatives
\mu_{R}^{N}
for
N,R\in\mathbb{N}
.
In Subsection
4.3
, we show the strong convergence of the density functions of
\mu_{R}^{N}
as
N\to\infty
for each fixed
R\in\mathbb{N}
.
Finally, in Subsection
4.4
, we construct the logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}
of
\mu
.

4.1.
L^{2}
-uniform bound of the logarithmic derivative
\mathsf{d}^{N}
of
\mu^{N}

Let
\mu^{N}
be a random point field satisfying
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}^{N}
be the labeled density function of
\mu^{N}
given by (
2.18
).
For
{x}=({x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\delta_{s^{i}}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{N}({x},\mathsf{s})=\nabla_{{x}}\log\mathfrak{m}^{N}({x},s^{1},\ldots,s^{N-1}),

(4.1)

where
\nabla_{{x}}=(\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}_{d}})
.
From (
2.18
) and (
4.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{N}({x},\mathsf{s})=-\beta\Big(\nabla\Phi^{N}({x})+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\nabla_{{x}}\Psi^{N}({x},s^{i})\Big).

(4.2)

Integration by parts yields, for any
\mathsf{h}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(O_{N})\otimes\mathscr{D}_{\bullet\mathrm{b}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{h}({x},\mathsf{s})\mathsf{d}^{N}({x},\mathsf{s})\mu^{N,[1]}(dxd\mathsf{s})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\nabla_{{x}}\mathsf{h}({x},\mathsf{s})\mu^{N,[1]}(dxd\mathsf{s}).

Here,
\mu^{N,[1]}
is the one-reduced Campbell measure of
\mu
, defined as

d\mu^{N,[1]}=\rho^{N,1}(x)\mu^{N}_{x}(d\mathsf{s})d{x}
, where
\rho^{N,1}
is the one-point correlation function of
\mu^{N}
and
\mu^{N}_{x}=\mu^{N}(\cdot+\delta_{{x}}|\mathsf{s}(\{{x}\}\geq 1))
is the reduced Palm measure conditioned at
{x}
.
Thus,
\mathsf{d}^{N}
is the
\bullet
-logarithmic derivative of
\mu
in the sense of Definition
2.2
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
.
Let
\mathsf{d}^{N}=(\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N})_{p=1}^{d}
and
\partial_{p}=\partial/\partial x_{p}
for
{x}=(x_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, for any
\mathsf{h}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(O_{N})\otimes\mathscr{D}_{\bullet\mathrm{b}}
,

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{h}\sum_{p=1}^{d}\partial_{p}\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N}d\mu^{N,[1]}=\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{h}\big(\Delta\Phi^{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\psi^{N}({x},s^{i})\big)d\mu^{N,[1]}.

(4.3)

Proof.

From
(
A1
)
, we have
\Phi^{N}({x})=\infty
for
{x}\notin\overline{O_{N}}
and
\Delta_{{x}}\Psi^{N}({x},{y})=-c_{\small\ref{;1a}}^{N}\delta_{{y}}({x})+\psi^{N}({x},{y})
on
O_{N}\times O_{N}
.
From
(
A1
)
and
(
A2
)
, we deduce
\mathfrak{m}^{N}(\mathbf{x})=0
if
{x}^{i}={x}^{j}
for some
i\neq j
, where
\mathbf{x}=(x^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
.
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{h}({x},\mathsf{s})\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\Delta_{x}\Psi^{N}({x},s^{i})d\mu^{N,[1]}=\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{h}({x},\mathsf{s})\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\psi^{N}({x},s^{i})d\mu^{N,[1]}.

(4.4)

Then, (
4.3
) follows immediately from (
4.2
) and (
4.4
).
∎

For each
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathsf{f}_{Q}\!:\!\mathbb{R}^{d}\!\to\!\mathbb{R}
be a cut-off function such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{Q}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\mathsf{f}_{Q}({x})=\begin{cases}1&{x}\in S_{Q}\\
0&{x}\notin S_{Q+1}\end{cases},\ 0<\mathsf{f}_{Q}({x})<1,\ {x}\in S_{Q+1}\backslash\overline{S}_{Q},

\displaystyle\lvert\nabla\log\mathsf{f}_{Q}\rvert\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad 0<\lvert\nabla\log\mathsf{f}_{Q}({x})\rvert,\ {x}\in S_{Q+1}\backslash\overline{S}_{Q}.

(4.5)

In equation (
4.5
), we adopt the convention such that

\displaystyle\text{$\lvert\nabla\log\mathsf{f}_{Q}\rvert=0$ on ${x}\notin S_{Q+1}$},\quad\lim_{{x}\to\partial S_{Q+1}}\lvert\nabla\log\mathsf{f}_{Q}({x})\rvert=0.

In the sequel, we regard a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}
by
f=f\otimes 1
.
Hence, for
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we set

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}fd\mu^{N,[1]}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}f\otimes 1d\mu^{N,[1]}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f({x})\rho^{N,1}({x})d{x},

where
\rho^{N,1}
is the one-point correlation function of
\mu^{N}
.
Note that
\rho^{N,1}
exists because
\mu^{N}
has a density according to (
2.18
).
It is not apparent that the limit random point field
\mu
has a one-point correlation function
\rho^{1}
.
Indeed, we will prove this in Theorem
4.1
.
For
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
c_{4.1}
,
c_{4.2}
, and
c_{4.3}
be the constants defined by

\displaystyle c_{\small\ref{;22b}}(N)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}\lvert\nabla\log\mathsf{f}_{Q}\rvert^{2}\mu^{N,[1]}(d{x}d\mathsf{s}),

\displaystyle c_{\small\ref{;22c}}(N)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}\big(\Delta\Phi^{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\psi^{N}({x},s^{i})\big)\mu^{N,[1]}(d{x}d\mathsf{s}),

\displaystyle c_{\small\ref{;22z}}=\limsup_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\frac{1}{4}\big\{\sqrt{c_{\small\ref{;22b}}(N_{\mathfrak{n}})}+\sqrt{c_{\small\ref{;22b}}(N_{\mathfrak{n}})+4\beta c_{\small\ref{;22c}}(N_{\mathfrak{n}})}\big\}^{2},

(4.6)

where
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
.
Note that
c_{\small\ref{;22z}}
only depends on
\mathsf{f}_{Q}
,
\beta
,
\Delta\Phi^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}
,
c_{\small\ref{;32!}}
, and
\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}},[1]}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, for each
Q
,
c_{\small\ref{;22z}}
is finite.

Proof.

From (
4.5
),
\{c_{\small\ref{;22b}}(N)\}
are nonnegative and bounded.
From (
2.13
) and
\psi^{N}\geq 0
,
c_{\small\ref{;22b}}(N)+4\beta c_{\small\ref{;22c}}(N)
are non-negative.
From
(
A1
)
, (
2.21
) and
\lim_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}=\mu
,
\{c_{\small\ref{;22c}}(N_{\mathfrak{n}})\}
are bounded.
Hence, from (
4.6
),
c_{\small\ref{;22z}}\geq 0
is finite.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\mathfrak{n}\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}|\mathsf{d}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}|^{2}d\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}},[1]}\leq c_{\small\ref{;22z}}.

(4.7)

Proof.

From
\mathsf{d}^{N}=(\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N})_{p=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, (
2.10
), and Lemma
4.1
, we have for
S_{Q}\subset O_{N}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}|\mathsf{d}^{N}|^{2}d\mu^{N,[1]}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\sum_{p=1}^{d}(\mathsf{f}_{Q}\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N})\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N}d\mu^{N,[1]}\quad\text{ by $\mathsf{d}^{N}=(\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N})_{p=1}^{d}$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\sum_{p=1}^{d}\partial_{p}(\mathsf{f}_{Q}\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N})d\mu^{N,[1]}\quad\text{ by \eqref{:10w}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\sum_{p=1}^{d}(\partial_{p}\mathsf{f}_{Q})\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N}d\mu^{N,[1]}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}\sum_{p=1}^{d}\partial_{p}\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N}d\mu^{N,[1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}\sum_{p=1}^{d}(\partial_{p}\log\mathsf{f}_{Q})\mathsf{d}_{p}^{N}d\mu^{N,[1]}+\beta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}\big(\Delta\Phi^{N}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\psi^{N}({x},s^{i})\big)d\mu^{N,[1]},

where, for the forth line, we used Lemma
4.1
.
Let

\displaystyle x_{\mathfrak{n}}=\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{f}_{Q}|\mathsf{d}^{N_{\mathfrak{n}}}|^{2}d\mu^{N_{\mathfrak{n}},[1]}\}^{1/2}.
