We will prove the remaining claims of i later.

From (
5.37
),
\lim_{R\to\infty}\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}=0
. From (
5.26
),
\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}=0
in
C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q})
.
Hence, we deduce (
2.34
).
Note that (
5.53
) holds for all
R\in\mathbb{N}
and that we have (
2.37
) from (
2.34
).
Hence, using (
5.53
) and (
2.37
), we have, for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{\mu}

\displaystyle({x},\mathsf{s})=\beta\Big(-\nabla\Phi({x})-\sum_{s^{i}\in\overline{S}_{R}}\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{I}(\ell)}\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}{x}^{\mathbf{i}}+\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}({x})\Big)\quad\text{ for all $R\in\mathbb{N}$}

\displaystyle=\beta\Big(-\nabla\Phi({x})+\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big(-\sum_{s^{i}\in\overline{S}_{R}}\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{I}(\ell)}\eta_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\mathbf{i}}{x}^{\mathbf{i}}+\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{\ell}({x})\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\beta\Big(-\nabla\Phi({x})+\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big(-\sum_{s^{i}\in\overline{S}_{R}}\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})\Big)\Big)\quad\text{ in $C^{1}(\overline{S}_{Q,\mathsf{s},\epsilon})$ by \eqref{:11'}}

for all
Q\in\mathbb{N}
and each
\epsilon>0
.
Hence, we obtain (
2.35
). Thus, we have obtained ii.

Because (
2.35
) holds for all
\epsilon>0
,
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})
has a
\mu^{[1]}
-version such that
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})
is locally Lipschitz continuous in
{x}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\neq}(\mathsf{s}):=\{{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d};{x}\neq s^{i}\}
.
Thus, we obtain i.

Using (
2.30
) and (
2.35
), we have, in
\mu^{[1]}
-a.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{\mu}({x},\mathsf{s})=

\displaystyle-\beta\nabla\Phi({x})-\beta\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum 1_{S_{R}}(s^{i})\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})\quad\text{ by \eqref{:11"}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\nabla\Phi({x})-\beta\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{i}\Big(1_{S_{R}}(s^{i})-1_{S_{R}}({x}-s^{i})\Big)\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})

\displaystyle-\beta\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{i}1_{S_{R}}({x}-s^{i})\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum 1_{S_{R}}({x}-s^{i})\nabla\Psi({x}-s^{i})\quad\text{ by \eqref{:11s}}.

This implies the first claim of iii.
The second claim is obvious from the first claim.
∎

6.
Non-collision of solutions of ISDEs with infinitely many particles

In this section, we prove the non-collision property of the stochastic dynamics for infinite-particle systems described by solutions of the ISDE (
2.41
).

Importantly, we do not rely on the properties of the associated quasi-regular Dirichlet forms, such as capacity.

The main result of this section (Theorem
6.1
) applies to weak solutions of the ISDE (
2.41
). These solutions are unrelated to Dirichlet forms. We will apply Theorem
6.1
to the solutions
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
and
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathsf{a}}
provided in Subsection
8.2
and Subsection
10.1
, which are constructed via the lower Dirichlet forms.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a weak solution of (
2.41
) that satisfies

\displaystyle E\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}1_{S_{R}}(X_{u}^{i})\lvert\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{i},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{i\diamond})\rvert^{2}du\Big]<\infty\quad\text{ for all $T,R\in\mathbb{N}$},

(6.1)

\displaystyle E[0\vee(-\log\rvert X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j}\rvert);X_{0}^{i},X_{0}^{j}\in S_{R}]<\infty\quad\text{ for all $i\neq j,R\in\mathbb{N}$}.

(6.2)

Let
\mathsf{X}_{t}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s}}=\{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}\,;\,\mathsf{s}(\{{x}\})\in\{0,1\}\text{ for all }{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle P(\mathsf{X}_{t}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{ for all }0\leq t<\infty)=1.

(6.3)

Proof.

Let
i\neq j\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.
Without loss of generality, we can assume
X_{0}^{i},X_{0}^{j}\in S_{R}
.
We divide the case into two parts:
d\geq 3
and
d=2
. Suppose
d\geq 3
. Let

\displaystyle\tau_{\epsilon}=T\wedge\inf\{t\geq 0;\lvert X_{t}^{i}-X_{t}^{j}\rvert\leq\epsilon\}\wedge\min_{k=i,j}\inf\{t\geq 0;\ X_{t}^{k}\not\in S_{R}\}.

Here, we suppress
i,j,R,T\in\mathbb{N}
from the notation of
\tau_{\epsilon}
.

Let
\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}=\sum_{(k,l)=(i,j),(j,i)}
.
From (
2.40
), (
2.41
), and Itô’s formula, we obtain

\displaystyle E[-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\rvert]-E[-\log\lvert X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j}\rvert]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\Big(\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}},\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{k},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{k\diamond})\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}du\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{d-2}{2}\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\Big(\mathfrak{a}(X_{u}^{k})\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}},\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}}\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}du\Big].

(6.4)

Note that, for
X_{0}^{i},X_{0}^{j}\in S_{R}
, we have

0\wedge(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\rvert)\geq-\log 2R
.
Combining this with (
2.39
) and (
6.4
), we have

\displaystyle E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\rvert)]+\frac{d-2}{2c_{\small\ref{;12}}}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{2}}du\Big]

\displaystyle\leq-E[0\wedge(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\rvert)]+E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j}\rvert)]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\Big(\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}},\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{k},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{k\diamond})\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}du\Big].

(6.5)

Let
c_{6.1}
and
c_{6.2}
be the positive constants defined by

\displaystyle c_{\small\ref{;K5}}=E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j}\rvert)]+\log(2R),

\displaystyle c_{\small\ref{;K5a}}=2E\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}1_{S_{R}}(X_{u}^{i})\lvert\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{i},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{i\diamond})\rvert^{2}du\Big]^{1/2}.

(6.6)

Then, we have
c_{\small\ref{;K5}}<\infty
and
c_{\small\ref{;K5a}}<\infty
from (
6.2
) and (
6.1
). Let

\displaystyle x_{\epsilon}=E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\rvert)],

\displaystyle y_{\epsilon}=E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{2}}du\Big].

(6.7)

Then, from (
6.5
), (
6.6
), (
6.7
), and the Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle x_{\epsilon}+\frac{d-2}{2c_{\small\ref{;12}}}y_{\epsilon}\leq c_{\small\ref{;K5}}+\sqrt{y_{\epsilon}}c_{\small\ref{;K5a}}.

(6.8)

Using (
6.8
) and
d\geq 3
, we have
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}x_{\epsilon}<\infty
and
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}y_{\epsilon}<\infty
.
Hence, from Fatou’s lemma and
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}x_{\epsilon}<\infty
, we have that, for each
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{j}\rvert)]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\rvert)]

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}x_{\epsilon}<\infty.

(6.9)

Hence, from Fatou’s lemma and (
6.9
), we obtain, for each
i\neq j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{\tau_{0}}^{i}-X_{\tau_{0}}^{j}\rvert);\tau_{0}>0]

\displaystyle\leq E[\liminf_{t\to\infty}\big(0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{j}\rvert)\big);\tau_{0}>0]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}E[0\vee(-\log\lvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{j}\rvert);\tau_{0}>0]<\infty.

(6.10)

Let
\tilde{\tau}_{R,T}^{i,j}=\inf\{0\leq t\leq T;X_{t}^{i}\notin S_{R}\text{ or }X_{t}^{j}\notin S_{R}\}
.
Then, from (
6.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle P(X_{t}^{i}=X_{t}^{j}\text{ for some }0<t<\tilde{\tau}_{R,T}^{i,j};\tau_{0}>0)=0

for all
R,T,i\neq j\in\mathbb{N}
. This implies (
6.3
) for
d\geq 3
.

We next suppose
d=2
.
Applying Itô’s formula to
\log(0\vee(-\log\lvert{x}\rvert))
instead of
-\log\lvert{x}\rvert
in (
6.4
) yields (
6.3
) for
d=2
in a similar fashion. For the sake of completeness, we present further details.
Let
v
be such that
v({x})=0\vee-\log\lvert{x}\rvert
for
{x}\neq 0
and
v(0)=\infty
.
Applying Itô’s formula to
\log v=\log(0\vee-\log\lvert{x}\rvert)
, we have

\displaystyle E[\log v(X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j})]-E[\log v(X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j})]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{v(X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l})}\Big(\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}},\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{k},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{k\diamond})\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}du\Big]

\displaystyle-\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{v(X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l})^{2}}\Big(\mathfrak{a}(X_{u}^{k})\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}},\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}}\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}du\Big].

(6.11)

From (
2.39
) and (
6.11
), we have

\displaystyle E[\log v(X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j})]+\frac{1}{c_{\small\ref{;12}}}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{v(X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j})^{2}\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{2}}du\Big]

\displaystyle\leq E[\log v(X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j})]

\displaystyle-\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{v(X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l})}\Big(\frac{X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}}{\lvert X_{u}^{k}-X_{u}^{l}\rvert^{2}},\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{k},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{k\diamond})\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}du\Big].

(6.12)

Let
x_{\epsilon},y_{\epsilon}\geq 0
be such that

\displaystyle x_{\epsilon}=E[\log v(X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j})],

\displaystyle y_{\epsilon}=E\Big[\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{v(X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j})^{2}\lvert X_{u}^{i}-X_{u}^{j}\rvert^{2}}du\Big].

(6.13)

We set
c_{6.3}=E[v(X_{0}^{i}-X_{0}^{j})]
.
Then,
c_{\small\ref{;K5c}}<\infty
by (
6.2
). From (
6.12
) and (
6.13
),

\displaystyle x_{\epsilon}+y_{\epsilon}\leq c_{\small\ref{;K5c}}+\sqrt{y_{\epsilon}}c_{\small\ref{;K5a}}.

(6.14)

Hence, from (
6.14
), we have
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}x_{\epsilon}<\infty
and
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}y_{\epsilon}<\infty
.
Then, using Fatou’s lemma and
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}x_{\epsilon}<\infty
,
we have that, for each
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle E[0\vee(-\log\rvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{0}}^{j}\lvert)]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}E[0\vee(-\log\rvert X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{i}-X_{t\wedge\tau_{\epsilon}}^{j}\lvert)]

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}x_{\epsilon}<\infty.

This corresponds to (
6.9
) for
d\geq 3
.
The rest of the proof for
d=2
is the same as the case
d=3
. Thus, we obtain (
6.3
) for
d=2
.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\mathbf{X}=(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a weak solution of (
2.41
). Assume (
6.1
) and

\displaystyle P\circ\mathbf{X}_{0}^{-1}\text{ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}$}.

(6.15)

Then, (
6.3
) holds.

Proof.

From (
6.1
), (
6.15
), and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle E\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}1_{S_{R}}(X_{u}^{i})\lvert\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{i},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{i\diamond})\rvert^{2}du|\mathbf{X}_{0}=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})\Big]<\infty\quad\text{ for $\mu$-a.s.\,$\mathsf{s}$}.

Hence, applying Theorem
6.1
to
\mathbf{X}
such that
\mathbf{X}_{0}=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})
, we have

\displaystyle P(\mathsf{X}_{t}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{ for all }0\leq t<\infty|\mathbf{X}_{0}=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s}))=1\quad\text{ for $\mu$-a.s.\,$\mathsf{s}$}.

(6.16)

Integrating the left-hand side of (
6.16
) with respect to
\mu\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}
, we obtain (
6.3
).
∎

Let
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}=(X^{1},\ldots,X^{m},\sum_{j>m}\delta_{X^{j}})
be an
m
-labeled process. Suppose that
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
is a weak solution of SDE (
2.41
) for
i=1,\ldots,m
:

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i}-X_{0}^{i}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{u}^{i})dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{i},\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{X_{u}^{j}})du\quad(1\leq i\leq m).

Let
\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{s})=(\mathfrak{l}^{1}(\mathsf{s}),\ldots,\mathfrak{l}^{m}(\mathsf{s}),\sum_{i>m}\delta_{\mathfrak{l}^{i}(\mathsf{s})})
for a label
\mathfrak{l}=(\mathfrak{l}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
2\leq m<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
be as above.
Assume that
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
satisfies

\displaystyle E\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{m}1_{S_{R}}(X_{u}^{i})\lvert\mathfrak{b}(X_{u}^{i},\mathsf{X}_{u}^{i\diamond})\rvert^{2}du\Big]<\infty\quad\text{ for all $T,R\in\mathbb{N}$},

\displaystyle P\circ(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{[m]})^{-1}\text{ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu\circ(\mathfrak{l}^{[m]})^{-1}$}.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{t}^{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{X_{t}^{i}}
.
Then, we have
P(\mathsf{X}_{t}^{m}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{ for all }0\leq t<\infty)=1
.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem
6.3
is the same as Theorem
6.2
, so is omitted.
∎

7.
Dirichlet Forms in Finite and Infinite Volume

A symmetric Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
on
L^{2}(\nu)
is a symmetric, closed, non-negative bilinear form that satisfies the Markov property
[
7
,
5
]
(see Definition
13.2
).

To prove results Theorem
2.2
–Theorem
2.5
, we employ the Dirichlet form approach
[
15
,
16
,
22
,
24
,
25
,
34
]
. We introduce the Dirichlet forms associated with the ISDE (
2.41
) and construct infinite-volume Dirichlet forms as limits of finite-volume forms. These finite-volume Dirichlet forms fall into two fundamentally different families, known as the lower and upper forms.

By utilizing the family of lower Dirichlet forms, we establish the closability of the limit infinite-volume Dirichlet form. Conversely, we will demonstrate the quasi-regularity of the upper Dirichlet form, which guarantees the existence of the associated diffusion process.

7.1.
Convergence of Dirichlet forms.

For
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}
, let
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}=\mu(\cdot|\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{s})=\pi_{\overline{S}_{R}^{c}}(\mathsf{y}))
as defined previously.
Let
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
be the
m
-reduced Campbell measure of
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}
.
Because the number of particles in
\overline{S}_{R}
is finite under
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}
, we can define
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
by (
2.46
) with the substitution of
\mu
for
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}
.
Let
\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
be the configuration space over
\overline{S}_{R}
.
Let
\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R}^{k}=\{\mathsf{s}\in\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R};\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R})=k\}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. For
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}
and each
R\in\mathbb{N}
, the following hold.

i

For
m,k\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
such that
m+k\geq 1
,

\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
has a bounded and continuous density
\mathfrak{m}_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
with respect to
\Lambda_{R}^{[m]}
on
\overline{S}_{R}^{m}\times\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R}^{k}
.

ii

\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}
has a one-point correlation function
\rho_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{1}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{m}_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{m+k}
be the
(m+k)
-labeled density of
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}
.
From (
5.18
) and the definition of the one-reduced Campbell measure, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\log\mathfrak{m}_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{m+k}({x}^{1},\ldots,{x}^{m+k})=\mathsf{d}_{R,\mathsf{y}}({x}^{i},\sum_{{x}^{j}\in\overline{S}_{R},j\neq i}^{m+k}\delta_{{x}^{j}})

(7.1)

in the distributional sense on
\overline{S}_{R}^{m+k}
with the test function space
C_{0}^{\infty}(S_{R}^{m+k})
.

From Theorem
5.2
, we have a representation of
\mathsf{d}_{R,\mathsf{y}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{R,\mathsf{y}}({x},\mathsf{s})=\beta\Big(-\nabla\Phi({x})-\sum_{s^{i}\in\overline{S}_{R}}\nabla\Psi({{x}-s^{i}})+\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{I}(\ell)}\eta_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{\mathbf{i}}{x}^{\mathbf{i}}+\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{\ell}({x})\Big).

(7.2)

Recall that
\mathscr{R}_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{\ell}\in C^{1}(\overline{S}_{R})
and that
\eta_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{\mathbf{i}}
are constants from Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
5.2
.
Hence, combining these with (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), we obtain i.

From (
2.22
),
\int_{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{s}(S_{R})\mu(d\mathsf{s})<\infty
.
Hence,
\int_{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{s}(S_{R})\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}(d\mathsf{s})<\infty
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}
.
From this, we can prove ii in a similar fashion as Theorem
4.1
ii.
∎

Remark 7.1
.

Leblé
[
19
, Th. 1]
proved the existence of continuous, local density of
\mu
in case of
d=2
.
The second author demonstrated that the existence of the logarithmic derivative implies the existence of the densities
[
37
]
. Based on this, along with Theorem
4.3
, we can establish the existence of the local density of
\mu
for all
d\geq 2
. In Theorem
7.1
, we provided a proof that is based on the explicit representation of the logarithmic derivative obtained in Theorem
2.1
.

Recall that
\mathsf{S}_{R}^{k}=\{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S};\mathsf{s}(S_{R})=k\}
.
Let
\mathfrak{a}=(\mathfrak{a}_{kl})_{k,l=1}^{d}
be the symmetric, matrix-valued function in (
2.39
).
Let
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}
be as in (
2.8
). For
f,g\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}
and
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}[f,g](\mathsf{s})=1_{\mathsf{S}_{R}^{k}}(\mathsf{s})\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s^{i}\in S_{R}}\Big(\mathfrak{a}\frac{\partial f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{k}}{\partial s^{i}},\frac{\partial g_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{k}}{\partial s^{i}}\Big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbf{x}_{R}^{k}(\mathsf{s})),

where
\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{i}}=(\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{i}_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{i}_{d}})
,

f_{R,\mathsf{s}}^{k}
is as in Definition
2.1
for
f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}
, and

\mathbf{x}_{R}^{k}(\mathsf{s})
is the
S_{R}^{k}
-coordinate of
\mathsf{s}
introduced after (
2.2
).
Note that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}[f,g](\mathsf{s})
is independent of the choice of
the
S_{R}^{k}
-coordinate
\mathbf{x}_{R}^{k}(\mathsf{s})
. Let

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}.

Then,
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}[f,f](\mathsf{s})
is non-decreasing in
R
for all
f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}
and
\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S}
.
Hence, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}[f,f](\mathsf{s})=\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}[f,f](\mathsf{s})\leq\infty.

(7.3)

We set the carré du champ
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}[f,g](\mathsf{s})
by polarization for

\mathsf{s}
such that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}[f,f](\mathsf{s})<\infty
and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}[g,g](\mathsf{s})<\infty
.

We consider the
m
-labeled space.
Let
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[0]}=\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{[0]}=\mathscr{D}_{\circ}
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[m]}=C_{0}^{\infty}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m})\otimes\mathscr{D}_{\bullet},\quad\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{[m]}=C_{0}^{\infty}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m})\otimes\mathscr{D}_{\circ}.

(7.4)

We introduce the
m
-labeled carré du champs on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}
.
For
m\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}^{[m]}[f,g]=\frac{1}{2}1_{S_{R}^{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(\mathfrak{a}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{i}}\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}[f,g],

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}^{[m]}[f,g]=\frac{1}{2}1_{S_{R}^{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(\mathfrak{a}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{i}}\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}[f,g],

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[m]}[f,g]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(\mathfrak{a}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{i}}\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}[f,g]\quad\text{ for $f,g\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[m]}$}.

(7.5)

For
m=0
, we set
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}^{[m]}=\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}
,
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}^{[0]}=\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}
, and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[0]}=\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}
.
Let
\mu^{[m]}
be the
m
-reduced Campbell measure of
\mu
defined by (
2.47
).
We set
\mu^{[0]}=\mu
. We set

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R}(f,g)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R}^{[m]}[f,g]d\mu^{[m]},

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[m]};\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R}(f,f)<\infty,\,f\in L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})\},

(7.7)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{R\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}};\text{ $f$ is $\mathscr{B}(S_{R}^{m})\times\sigma[\pi_{S_{R}}]$-measurable}\}.

(7.8)

Although
\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R}
,
\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}
, and
\mathscr{D}_{R\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}}
depend on
\mathfrak{a}
, we omit
\mathfrak{a}
from the notation.
Replacing
\mu^{[m]}
by
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
, we set
\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}}
and
\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}}
.

For non-negative symmetric bilinear forms
(\mathscr{E}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{1})
and
(\mathscr{E}_{2},\mathscr{D}_{2})
on
L^{2}(\nu)
, we say
(\mathscr{E}_{2},\mathscr{D}_{2})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{1})
if

\mathscr{D}_{1}\subset\mathscr{D}_{2}
and
\mathscr{E}_{1}(f,f)=\mathscr{E}_{2}(f,f)
for all
f\in\mathscr{D}_{1}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Let
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

i

(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}
.

ii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

iii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

Proof.

From Theorem
7.1
,
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
has a bounded and continuous density.
Hence,
(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
.
This follows from
[
22
, Lem. 3.2]
for
m=0
. The generalization to
m\geq 1
is straightforward.
Thus, we obtain i.

From i, we have the superposition of the closures of

(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
(see (
11.1
)).
From the general theorem on the superposition of closed forms (cf.
[
4
, Prop. 3.1.1]
), the superposition is a closed form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Because the superposition is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
, we obtain ii from Lemma
13.4
.
Clearly,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
Hence, from Lemma
13.4
, iii follows from ii.
∎

Remark 7.2
.

In
[
37
]
, the second named author proved that the existence of logarithmic derivative implies the closability of bilinear forms in Lemma
7.1
. This result is a general theory applicable to all random point fields
\nu
with logarithmic derivative.
Using this, we can prove Lemma
7.1
. We present here direct proof using the explicit representation of the logarithmic derivative for
\mu
in Theorem
2.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
be the closure of
(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
. Let
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
and
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
be
the closures of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
and
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\mathscr{D}_{R\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
, respectively.
It is clear that these closed forms are symmetric Dirichlet forms (cf. Definition
13.2
). In the following lemma, we check that these forms are strongly local and quasi-regular. See Subsection
13.2
for the concept of a strongly local, quasi-regular Dirichlet form.

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
.
Let
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

i

(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})

is a strongly local, quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
for
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{y}
, where we regard
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
as a measure on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}
.

ii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a strongly local, quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

iii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a strongly local Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}^{[m]}
be as in (
7.5
). We set

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]}(f,g)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a},R,k}^{[m]}[f,g]d\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}.

Then,
(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
is the countable sum of the closed forms

(\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]})
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]},\quad\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}}=\{f\in\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]};\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]}(f,f)<\infty\}.

(7.9)

Here, each closed form
(\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]})
is the closure of

(\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]},\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
.
Note that, we impose the reflecting boundary condition to
(\mathscr{E}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R,\mathsf{y},k}^{[m]})
,
which is a strongly local, quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
.

Let
\mathrm{Cnf}_{R,k}^{[m]}=\{({x},\mathsf{s})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S};{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m},\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R})=k\}
.
The state space of the diffusion properly associated with
(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
is
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}

and can be decomposed as
\mathrm{Cnf}_{R,k}^{[m]}
,
k\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.
Note that, for each
k
, the dynamics are frozen outside
\mathrm{Cnf}_{R,k}^{[m]}
and that
\mathrm{Cnf}_{R,k}^{[m]}
is invariant under the dynamics of the diffusion above.
Furthermore,
\mathrm{Cnf}_{R,k}^{[m]}
are mutually disjoint for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, from (
7.9
),
(\mathscr{E}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}})
is strongly local and quasi-regular on
L^{2}(\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]})
. This completes the proof of i.

We can prove ii in a similar manner to i. Because the carré du champ consists of differential,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is strongly local. Thus, we have iii.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

The concept of quasi-regularity depends on the choice of state space.
In Lemma
7.2
i, we took the state space as
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}
.
We can take the state space as
\overline{S}_{R}^{m}\times\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
, where we can regard
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}^{[m]}
as a measure on
\overline{S}_{R}^{m}\times\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
and particles outside
\overline{S}_{R}^{m}\times\overline{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
are regarded as frozen random environment.

Next, we examine the infinite-volume Dirichlet forms.
For
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}(f,g)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[m]}[f,g]d\mu^{[m]},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\{f\in\bigcap_{R=1}^{\infty}\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}};\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R}(f,f)<\infty\}.

(7.10)

Clearly,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R+1},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R+1}^{\mu^{[m]}})
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R}(f,g)=\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R+1}(f,g)\quad\text{ for all }f,g\in\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R+1}^{\mu^{[m]}},\quad\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}}\supset\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R+1}^{\mu^{[m]}}.

(7.11)

Hence, from (
7.3
), (
7.6
), (
7.10
), and (
7.11
),
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the increasing limit of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
in the sense of (
13.2
).
The convergence of the strong resolvent sense is defined before Lemma
13.5
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, the following hold for
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

i

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a closed form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
The closed form

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the limit of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
,
R\in\mathbb{N}
,
in the strong resolvent sense on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

ii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\cup_{R\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
The closure
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the limit of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
,
R\in\mathbb{N}
,
in the strong resolvent sense on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

Proof.

Because
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the increasing limit of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
, we obtain i
from Lemma
13.5
i.

Because
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\cup_{R\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
, the first claim of ii follows from i and Lemma
13.4
.
Clearly,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R+1},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R+1}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
Hence,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is decreasing with the limit
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\cup_{R\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
By definition,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the closure of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\cup_{R\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Hence, ii follows from Lemma
13.5
ii.
∎

7.2.
Dirichlet forms in infinite volume.

We now introduce new bilinear forms in addition to the ones we had before.

Let
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[m]}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{[m]}
be as in (
7.4
). Recall that
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[m]}\supset\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{[m]}
. We set

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{[m]};\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}(f,f)<\infty,\,f\in L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\{f\in\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{[m]};\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}(f,f)<\infty,\,f\in L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})\}.

(7.12)

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, for each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, the following hold.

i

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

ii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

Proof.

From (
7.3
), (
7.7
), and (
7.11
),
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
By Lemma
7.3
i,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a closed form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Hence, i follows from this and Lemma
13.4
.
Note that
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
Hence, ii follows from i and Lemma
13.4
.
∎

Let
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\bullet})
and
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\circ})
be the closures of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}})
and
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
, respectively.

Lemma 7.5
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, for each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, the following hold.

i

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\bullet})=(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.

ii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\circ})=(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.

Proof.

Because
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}\subset\mathscr{D}_{R\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}
for all
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}(f,f)<\infty
for all
f\in\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}
,
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}\subset\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}
.
It is not difficult to show that
\mathscr{D}_{\bullet}^{\mu^{[m]}}
is dense in
\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}
.
This yields i.

From Lemma
7.3
ii,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the closure of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\cup_{R\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Note that
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\cup_{R\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
Hence,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is an extension of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
From
[
15
, Lem. 2.5]
,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is dense in
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
Hence, we have ii.
∎

We refer to
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
and
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
as the lower and upper Dirichlet forms for
\mu^{[m]}
, respectively.

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, the following hold for each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

i

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a strongly local Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

ii

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a strongly local, quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of Dirichlet forms (see Definition
13.2
).
From Lemma
7.3
i,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a closed form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Clearly,
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[m]}[f,g]=\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[m]}[g,f]
for all
f,g\in\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}
, which implies that
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is symmetric.
From (
7.10
),

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}(f,g)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[m]}[f,g]d\mu^{[m]}.

Because
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{[m]}
is the carré du champ consisting of differentials,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
satisfies (
13.3
) and the strong locality property. Thus, we obtain i.

According to Theorem
13.3
,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\circ})
is a quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
From Lemma
7.5
ii,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\circ})=(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
, which implies
the quasi-regularity of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
The strong locality of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
follows from (
7.10
), and the definition of
\mathbb{D}_{\mathfrak{a}}
given by (
7.5
).
∎

8.
Weak solutions of ISDE for the lower Dirichlet form

The purpose of Section
8
is to construct a weak solution
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
to the ISDE (
2.41
) associated with the lower Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

In Section
8.1
, we construct the
m
-labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
, which is properly associated with the lower Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
. Utilizing
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
, we then construct the fully labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
and demonstrate that
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
is a weak solution of (
2.58
) and (
2.59
) (Lemma
8.1
).
Furthermore, we define the fully labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
as the limit of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
, and show that the
m
-labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
, derived from
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
, is associated with the lower Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
(Theorem
8.1
).

In Section
8.2
, we establish that
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
indeed solves the ISDE (
2.41
) (Theorem
8.2
).

8.1.
The
m
-labeled process for the lower Dirichlet form.

We recall several concepts of Dirichlet form theory.
A Markov process
\{X_{t}\}
is said to be associated with the Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
on
L^{2}(\nu)
if
\{X_{t}\}
is associated with the
L^{2}
-Markovian semi-group
T_{t}
given by
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
on
L^{2}(\nu)
in the sense that
E_{x}[f(X_{t})]=T_{t}f(x)
for
\nu
-a.s.
{x}
and for all
t\geq 0
and
f\in L^{2}(\nu)
.
We say that
\{X_{t}\}
is properly associated with
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
on
L^{2}(\nu)
if, in addition,
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
is quasi-regular and
E_{x}[f(X_{t})]
is a quasi-continuous
\nu
-version of
T_{t}f(x)
.
If
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{D})
is strongly local, then the associated Markov process becomes a diffusion, that is, a continuous Markov process with the strong Markov property. See Subsection
13.2
or
[
5
]
for strong locality and quasi-continuity.

Let
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
be the quasi-regular, strongly local Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
as in Lemma
7.2
.
Let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
be the diffusion properly associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Let
\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})=(\mathfrak{l}^{i}(\mathsf{s}))_{i}
be the label defined as (
2.43
).
For
\mathsf{s}(\overline{S}_{R})\geq m
, we take

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}(0)=(\mathfrak{l}^{1}(\mathsf{s}),\ldots,\mathfrak{l}^{m}(\mathsf{s}),\sum_{i>m}\delta_{\mathfrak{l}^{i}(\mathsf{s})}).

Let
W^{[m]}:=C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S})

(m\geq 1)
and
W^{[0]}:=C([0,\infty);\mathsf{S})
.
Let
\mathbf{w}^{[m]}=(w^{1}\ldots,w^{m},\mathsf{w}^{m*})\in W^{[m]}
, where

\mathsf{w}^{m*}(t)=\sum_{j>m}\delta_{w^{j}(t)}
for
\mathbf{w}=(w^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Let
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m,n]}\!:\!W^{[n]}\!\to\!W^{[m]}
for
m\leq n
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m,n]}(\mathbf{w}^{[n]})=(w^{1},\ldots,w^{m},\sum_{m<i\leq n}\delta_{w^{i}}+\mathsf{w}^{n*}).

Let
\underline{P}_{R}^{[m]}
be the distribution of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
.
We can easily show that
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
,
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, are consistent in the sense that

\displaystyle\underline{P}_{R}^{[m]}=\underline{P}_{R}^{[n]}\circ(\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m,n]})^{-1}\quad\text{ for all }0\leq m\leq n<\infty.

(8.1)

We take the space where
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
is defined as
W^{[m]}
.
From (
8.1
), we can write
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}=(\underline{X}_{R}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Here,
\underline{X}_{R}^{i}=w^{i}
and
(w^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
.
We set
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}=(\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m},\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*})
, where

\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m}=(\underline{X}_{R}^{i})_{i=1}^{m}
and
\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}(t)=\sum_{i>m}^{\infty}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)}
.

Let
(B^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of independent
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motions.
Let
r_{t}
be the time-reversal operator on the path space on
[0,\infty)
such that

r_{t}(\omega)(s)=\omega(t-s)
if
0\leq s\leq t
and
r_{t}(\omega)(s)=\omega(0)
if
t\leq s
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, for all
R\in\mathbb{N}
, the following hold.

i

\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
is a weak solution of SDE (
2.58
) and

\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}(t)=\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}(0)
for all
t\geq 0
.

ii

\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
is a weak solution of ISDE (
2.58
) and (
2.59
).

iii

Suppose (
2.63
). Then, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\ \underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\circ r_{t}\Big\}.

(8.2)

iv

Let
\mathscr{C}^{[m]}=\{({x},\mathsf{s})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S};{x}=s^{i}\text{ for some }i\}
, where
\mathsf{s}=\sum_{i}\delta_{s^{i}}
. Then, the capacity of
\mathscr{C}^{[m]}
with respect to
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
equals zero.

Proof.

Let
m
be the number of particles of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}(0)
in
\overline{S}_{R}
.

The Fukushima decomposition is the counterpart of Itô’s formula in the theory of Dirichlet forms
[
5
, Th. 4.2.6]
. Because
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
, we can use the Fukushima decomposition.
We regard
x^{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, as a function on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}
in an obvious manner.
Applying the Fukushima decomposition to
x^{i}
, we see that
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m}=(\underline{X}_{R}^{i})_{i=1}^{m}
satisfies

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)=M_{t}^{[x^{i}]}+N_{t}^{[x^{i}]},\quad\text{ $1\leq i\leq m$},

(8.3)

where
M^{[x^{i}]}
is the martingale additive functional of finite energy and

N^{[x^{i}]}
is the continuous additive functional of zero energy of the Fukushima decomposition for the additive functional
A_{t}^{[x^{i}]}=\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)
.

Using a straightforward calculation, we obtain that (cf.
[
5
, pp.164-165]
)

\displaystyle M_{t}^{[x^{i}]}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}

(8.4)

and that the zero energy additive functional
N^{[x^{i}]}
satisfies

\displaystyle N_{t}^{[x^{i}]}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{j}(u)})du+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u).

(8.5)

We can prove (
8.5
) similarly to
[
7
, Example 5.2.2]
. In
[
7
, Example 5.2.2]
, it was assumed that
\mathfrak{a}
is the unit matrix; however, generalizing to the current case is straightforward.
From (
8.4
) and (
8.5
),
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m}
satisfies (
2.58
).
Because
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
has no energy outside
\overline{S}_{R}
, we deduce
\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}(t)=\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}(0)
for all
t\geq 0
. Thus, we obtain i.

We next consider
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}=(\underline{X}_{R}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. From (
8.3
)–(
8.5
),
\underline{X}_{R}^{i}

satisfy (
2.59
) for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
For
i>m
, SDE (
2.59
) obviously holds because

\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}(t)=\sum_{i>m}^{\infty}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)}
is frozen outside
\overline{S}_{R}
under the dynamics of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
This implies ii.

From the Lyons–Zheng decomposition
[
5
, Th. 6.7.2]
, we write
\underline{X}_{R}^{i}
as the sum of
the martingale additive functionals:

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\{M_{t}^{[x^{i}]}-M_{t}^{[x^{i}]}\circ r_{t}\Big\}.

(8.6)

Indeed, from
[
5
, Th. 6.7.2]
, (
8.6
) holds
P_{\mu^{[m]}}
-a.e. up to lifetime, where
P_{\mu^{[m]}}
is the distribution of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
satisfying
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}\mu^{[m]}
. Then, from (
2.63
), the distribution of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{\mu^{[m]}}
. Thus, (
8.6
) holds.

From (
8.4
) and (
8.6
), we obtain (
8.2
). This implies iii.

Clearly,
\mu^{[m]}(\mathscr{C}^{[m]})=0
.
Because the
m
-density function of
\mu_{R,\mathsf{y}}
is bounded on
\underline{S}_{R}^{m}
,
d\geq 2
, and
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
has no energy outside
\overline{S}_{R}
, we obtain iv.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
, (
2.61
), and (
2.63
). Then, the following hold:

\displaystyle\sup_{R,i\in\mathbb{N}}E[\lvert\ \underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u)\rvert^{4}]\leq c_{\small\ref{;76}}\lvert t-u\rvert^{2},\text{ }0\leq t,u<\infty,

(8.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to\infty}\liminf_{R\to\infty}P(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\ \underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)\rvert\leq a)=1\quad\text{ for each }m,T\in\mathbb{N},

(8.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}\inf_{R\in\mathbb{N}}P(I_{R,T}(\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R})\leq l)=1\quad\text{ for each }T\in\mathbb{N}.

(8.9)

Here,
c_{8.1}>0
is a constant.

Proof.

From (
2.61
) and (
2.63
), we can assume
\varphi=1
without loss of generality.

We have

\langle\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))du
and
\sigma^{t}\sigma=\mathfrak{a}
. From (
2.39
),
\mathfrak{a}
is uniformly elliptic and bounded.
Hence, from (
8.2
) and the martingale inequality, we have (
8.7
)–(
8.9
).
The proof of (
8.9
) is not as easy as other claims.
We can prove (
8.9
) in the same fashion as Lemma 8.7 in
[
34
, pp. 1212–1214]
and the detail is omitted here.
∎

For a process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}=(\underline{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, we set
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}=(\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{m},\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{m*})
,
where
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{m}=(\underline{X}^{i})_{i=1}^{m}
and
\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{m*}=\sum_{i>m}^{\infty}\delta_{\underline{X}^{i}}
.
Let
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
be the Dirichlet form as in Lemma
7.6
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
, (
2.61
), and (
2.63
). Then, there exists a fully labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
satisfying the following.

i

For each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, the
m
-labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
is the
\mu^{[m]}
-symmetric and conservative, continuous Markov process associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

ii

For each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, the
m
-labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
satisfies

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}\quad\text{ in distribution in $W^{[m]}$}.

(8.10)

iii

The fully labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{X}}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}\quad\text{ in distribution in $C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}$}.

(8.11)

In particular, each tagged particle
\underline{X}^{i}
of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}=(\underline{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
does not explode.

Proof.

Let
W^{m}=C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}
and
W^{\mathbb{N}}=C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
with the product topology.
From (
2.63
),
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}(\varphi d\mu)\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}
.
Combining this with (
8.7
), we deduce the tightness of
\ \underline{X}_{R}^{i}
in
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, we obtain the tightness of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m}
and
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
in
W^{m}
and
W^{\mathbb{N}}=C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
, respectively, because

W^{m}
and
W^{\mathbb{N}}
are endowed with the product topology.

Combining the tightness of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
in
W^{\mathbb{N}}
with
(
8.9
) and using Lemma
13.3
, we obtain that
\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*}
is tight in
C([0,\infty);\mathsf{S})
.
Hence,
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}=(\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m},\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*})
is tight in
W^{[m]}
.

Let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
be any limit point of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}
.
According to Lemma
7.3
i,
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}
converges to
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
in distribution in
W^{[m]}

and
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
is a
\mu^{[m]}
-symmetric and continuous Markov process associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
In particular,
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
are unique in distribution and the convergence in (
8.10
) holds.
Because
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[m]}=(\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{m},\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{m*})
is tight in
W^{[m]}
, the limit continuous Markov process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
in (
8.10
) is conservative.

Because (
8.10
) holds for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the convergence in (
8.11
) holds and
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
is unique in distribution.
The second statement of iii follows from the first because
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
can be regard as a
W^{\mathbb{N}}
-valued random variable from (
8.11
).
∎

8.2.
Weak solutions of ISDEs for the lower Dirichlet form.

Let
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
be as in Theorem
8.1
. The goal of Subsection
8.2
is to prove that
\underline{\mathbf{X}}=(\underline{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a weak solution of ISDE (
2.41
) satisfying
(
SIN
)
,
(
NBJ
)
, and
(
AC
)
μ
.

Let
\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}
be as in Lemma
8.1
. Let

\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u)=\sum_{j\not=i}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{j}(u)}
and

\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u)=\sum_{j\not=i}\delta_{\underline{X}^{j}(u)}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Make the same assumption as Theorem
8.1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}},

(8.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}((\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u)))du\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

(8.13)

in distribution in
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Here,
\sigma\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a matrix such that
\sigma^{t}\sigma=\mathfrak{a}
, and
\mathfrak{a}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\mathfrak{b}=\frac{1}{2}\{\nabla\mathfrak{a}+\mathsf{d}^{\mu}\mathfrak{a}\}
are as in (
2.39
) and (
2.40
).

Proof.

Let
\langle M\rangle_{t}
be the quadratic process of continuous martingale
M_{t}
.
Then, from (
8.11
),
\sigma^{t}\sigma=\mathfrak{a}
, and
\mathfrak{a}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we obtain, for all
0\leq t<\infty
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big(\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\rangle_{t}\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))du\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}^{i}(u))du\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=\Big(\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\rangle_{t}\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}.

(8.14)

From (
8.14
), we obtain (
8.12
) because the convergence in distribution in
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
of continuous martingale follows from that of the associated quadratic process.

From Theorem
2.1
, we have
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}(\mu^{[1]})
.
Then,
\mathfrak{b}_{Q}:=1_{\overline{S}_{Q}}\mathfrak{b}\in L^{2}(\mu^{[1]})
for any
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S})
such that

\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}=1_{\overline{S}_{Q}}\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}
and

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{b}_{Q}-\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mu^{[1]})}<\epsilon.

(8.15)

Hence, for any
T,i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle E\big[\int_{0}^{T}\big|(\mathfrak{b}_{Q}-\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon})(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))\big|du\big]\leq E\big[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big|(\mathfrak{b}_{Q}-\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon})(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))\big|du\big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{\small\ref{;14}}T\mu^{[1]}(S_{Q}\times\mathsf{S})^{1/2}\|(\mathfrak{b}_{Q}-\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon})\|_{L^{2}(\mu^{[1]})}<c_{\small\ref{;14}}T\mu^{[1]}(S_{Q}\times\mathsf{S})^{1/2}\epsilon.

(8.16)

Here, we used (
2.61
), (
2.63
), that
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}^{[1]}
is a
\mu^{[1]}
-symmetric Markov process, and (
8.15
). Similarly, we have, for any
T,i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle E\big[\int_{0}^{T}\big|(\mathfrak{b}_{Q}-\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon})(\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u))\big|du\big]<c_{\small\ref{;14}}T\mu^{[1]}(S_{Q}\times\mathsf{S})^{1/2}\epsilon.

(8.17)

Note that
\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S})
. Then, we have, for any
F\in C_{b}(C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,

\displaystyle F\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}(w(u),\mathsf{w}(u))du\Big)\in C_{b}(C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S})).

(8.18)

Hence, from (
8.11
) and (
8.18
), we obtain, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}E\Big[F\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du\Big)\Big]=E\Big[F\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}_{Q}^{\epsilon}(\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u))du\Big)\Big].

(8.19)

From (
8.16
), (
8.17
), and (
8.19
), we easily obtain (
8.13
).
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Make the same assumption as Theorem
8.1
. Then, for each
i,T\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u)\Big\rvert>0\Big)=0.

(8.20)

Proof.

From (
8.11
),
\{\underline{X}_{Q}^{i}(\cdot)-\underline{X}_{Q}^{i}(0)\}_{Q\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)\rvert\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{R\to\infty}P\Big(\sup_{Q\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\underline{X}_{Q}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{Q}^{i}(0)\rvert\big)\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big)=0.

(8.21)

From Lemma
8.3
,
\{\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}_{Q}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\}_{Q\in\mathbb{N}}
and

\{\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{Q}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{Q}^{i\diamond}(u))du\}_{Q\in\mathbb{N}}
are tight in
C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, similarly as (
8.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}du\rvert\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du\rvert\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big)=0.

(8.22)

From ISDE (
2.58
) and
\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u)=\sum_{j\not=i}\delta_{\underline{X}_{R}^{j}(u)}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u)=

\displaystyle\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du.

(8.23)

Note that
L_{R}^{i}
only increases when
\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u)
is on the boundary
\partial S_{R}
from the second equation of (
2.58
).
Hence, from this and (
8.21
)–(
8.23
), we obtain

\displaystyle P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u)\Big\rvert>0\Big)\leq P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)\rvert\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big)

\displaystyle+P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}du\rvert\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big)+P\Big(\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du\rvert\geq\frac{R}{3}\Big).

Taking
R\to\infty
and using (
8.22
), we obtain (
8.20
).
∎

Let
\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i}
and
\mathbb{X}^{i}
be
C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4})
-valued random variables such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i}

\displaystyle=\Big(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(\cdot)-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0),\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i},\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du,\int_{0}^{\cdot}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u)\Big),

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}^{i}

\displaystyle=\Big(\underline{X}^{i}(\cdot)-\underline{X}^{i}(0),\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i},\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u))du,\mathbf{0}\Big).

(8.24)

Lemma 8.5
.

Make the same assumption as Theorem
8.1
. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=\lim_{R\to\infty}

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\quad\text{ in distribution in $C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4})^{\mathbb{N}}$}.

(8.25)

Proof.

We can rewrite
(\mathbb{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
as the
C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}})^{4}
-valued process:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}=(\underline{\mathbf{X}}(\cdot)-\underline{\mathbf{X}}(0),\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}},\Big(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{b}((\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u)))du\Big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}},(\mathbf{0})_{i\in\mathbb{N}})

We rewrite
(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
similarly.
The convergence of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}(\cdot)-\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}(0)
to
\underline{\mathbf{X}}(\cdot)-\underline{\mathbf{X}}(0)
follows from (
8.11
) and (
2.63
).
The convergence of the other terms follows from Lemma
8.3
and Lemma
8.4
.
From these, we easily obtain (
8.25
).
∎

Theorem 8.2
.

Make the same assumption as Theorem
8.1
. Then,
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
is a weak solution of (
2.41
)
satisfying
\underline{\mathbf{X}}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}(\varphi\mu)\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}
,
(
SIN
)
,
(
NBJ
)
, and
(
AC
)
μ
.

Proof.

Let
\{h_{k}\}
be an increasing sequence of positive numbers such that
\lim_{k}h_{k}=\infty
.
Let
S_{h_{k}}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{d};\lvert s\rvert<h_{k}\}
.
For
\mathbf{w}=(w_{i})_{i=1}^{4}\in C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4})
, let

\displaystyle\tau_{k}(\mathbf{w})=\inf\{t>0;\mathbf{w}(t)\notin S_{h_{k}}^{4}\}.

(8.26)

Let
\mathbf{V}_{k}
be the set of continuity points of the map
\mathbf{w}\mapsto\tau_{k}(\mathbf{w})
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{k}=\{\mathbf{w}\in C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4});\mathbf{w}\mapsto\tau_{k}(\mathbf{w})\text{ is continuous at }\mathbf{w}\}.

We can and do take
\{h_{k}\}
such that

\displaystyle P(\mathbb{X}^{i}\in\mathbf{V}_{k})=1\quad\text{ for all $i,k\in\mathbb{N}$}.

(8.27)

Let
F_{k}\in C_{0}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4})
such that

F_{k}({x},{y},{z},u)={x}-{y}-{z}-u
if
({x},{y},{z},u)\in S_{h_{k}}^{4}
.
Using Lemma
8.5
and (
8.27
), we obtain, for each
i,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle F_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i}(t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i})))=\lim_{R\to\infty}F_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i}(t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})))\ \text{ in distribution in $\mathbb{R}$}.

(8.28)

Recall that
\underline{\mathbf{X}}_{R}=(\underline{X}_{R}^{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
satisfies SDE (
2.58
).
Then, from the definition of
F_{k}
, (
8.24
), (
8.26
), and (
2.58
), we obtain, for each
R,i,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle F_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i}(t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})))=\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i}))-\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(0)-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})}\sigma(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}_{R}^{i\diamond}(u))du-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}_{R}^{i})}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}_{R}\mathfrak{a}(\underline{X}_{R}^{i}(u))dL_{R}^{i}(u)=0.

(8.29)

From (
8.28
) and (
8.29
), we obtain, for each
i,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle F_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i}(t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i})))=0,

which implies that

\displaystyle\underline{X}^{i}(t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i}))-\underline{X}^{i}(0)-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i})}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i})}\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{\mathsf{X}}^{i\diamond}(u))du=0.

From (
8.24
) and Lemma
8.5
, the equation above holds as the identity of continuous processes.
Hence, using
\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau_{k}(\mathbb{X}^{i})=\infty
, we obtain ISDE (
2.41
).

We proceed with
(
SIN
)
. Let
W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})
be as in (
2.53
).
We will prove
\underline{\mathbf{X}}\in W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})
a.s., that is, each tagged particle neither explodes nor collides with any other particle.

From Theorem
8.1
iii,
\underline{X}^{i}
does not explode for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

To prove the non-collision property of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
, we use Theorem
6.2
.
From Theorem
2.1
,
\mathsf{d}^{\mu}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2}(\mu^{[1]})
.
From Theorem
8.1
, the one-labeled process
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[1]}
given by
\underline{\mathbf{X}}
is a
\mu^{[1]}
-symmetric Markov process.
Hence, the
\mu^{[1]}
-symmetry of
\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{[1]}
implies that

\displaystyle E\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}1_{S_{R}}(\underline{X}^{i}(u))\lvert\mathfrak{b}(\underline{X}^{i}(u),\underline{X}^{i\diamond}(u))\rvert^{2}du\Big]

\displaystyle\leq c_{\small\ref{;14}}T\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{S}}1_{S_{R}}({x})\lvert\mathfrak{b}({x},\mathsf{s})\rvert^{2}d\mu^{[1]}<\infty

(8.30)

for all
R,T\in\mathbb{N}
.
Here, we have used
0\leq\varphi\leq c_{\small\ref{;14}}
, where
\underline{\mathbf{X}}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}(\varphi\mu)\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}(d\mathbf{x})
from (
2.63
). From (
8.30
), we obtain (
6.1
).
Note that (
6.15
) follows from
\underline{\mathbf{X}}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}(\varphi\mu)\circ\mathfrak{l}^{-1}(d\mathbf{x})
.
Thus, we have verified the assumptions (
6.1
) and (
6.15
) of Theorem
6.2
.
Hence, we obtain the non-collision property (
6.3
).
Thus, we obtain
(
SIN
)
.

From (
8.2
) and Theorem
8.1
, we have, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\underline{X}^{i}(t)-\underline{X}^{i}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\underline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\circ r_{t}\Big\}.

(8.31)

From (
2.39
), (
8.31
), and the martingale inequality, we have
(
NBJ
)
.
Indeed, we can prove
(
NBJ
)
in the same fashion as Lemma 8.7 in
[
34
, pp. 1212–1214]
.
Assumption 8.81 in
[
34
]
follows from (
2.22
) in the present paper.
The detail is omitted here.

Assumption

(
AC
)
μ
is obvious because
\underline{\mathsf{X}}
is associated with the reversible Dirichlet form

(\mathscr{E}^{\mu},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu})
on
L^{2}(\mu)
and
\underline{\mathsf{X}}(0)\sim_{\mathrm{law}}\varphi\mu
.
∎

9.
Weak solutions of ISDEs for the upper Dirichlet form: Proof of Theorem
2.2

The aim of Section
9
is to prove Theorem
2.2
.
In Section
9.1
, we construct the
m
-labeled diffusion
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
.
In Section
9.2
, we then construct the associated fully labeled process
\overline{\mathbf{X}}
and demonstrate that
\overline{\mathbf{X}}
is a weak solution of (
2.41
) satisfying Assumptions
(
SIN
)
,
(
NBJ
)
, and
(
AC
)
μ
.
These conditions are essential for establishing pathwise uniqueness and the existence of strong solutions for the ISDE (
2.41
).

Unlike in Section
8
, the upper Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is quasi-regular. Therefore, we construct the
m
-labeled diffusion process
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
directly from the infinite volume upper Dirichlet form. This approach is markedly different from the finite volume approximation method used in Section
8
.

9.1.
The
m
-labeled process for the upper Dirichlet form.

Let
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
be the Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
as in Lemma
7.6
.
Let
\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu^{[m]}}
denote the capacity with respect to
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
We write
\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu}
for
m=0
.
Define
\mathfrak{u}^{[m,0]}\!:\!(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}\!\to\!\mathsf{S}
by
\mathfrak{u}^{[m,0]}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{m},\mathsf{s})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{x^{i}}+\mathsf{s}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
A\subset\mathsf{S}
.
Then
\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu}(A)=0
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu^{[m]}}((\mathfrak{u}^{[m,0]})^{-1}(A))=0\quad\text{ for all $m\in\mathbb{N}$}.

Proof.

The proof follows from the argument of
[
24
, Lem. 4.1]
.
∎

Theorem 9.1
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then, the following hold for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

i

There exists a
\mu^{[m]}
-symmetric, conservative diffusion
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
properly associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
for each
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
.

ii

We write
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}=(\overline{X}^{1},\ldots,\overline{X}^{m},\sum_{j>m}^{\infty}\delta_{\overline{X}^{j}})
.
None of the labeled particles
\overline{X}^{i}
, where
i=1,\ldots,m
, in
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
explode or collide with other labeled particles:

\displaystyle P(\overline{X}^{i}\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d}),i=1,\ldots,m)=1,

(9.1)

\displaystyle P(\overline{X}^{i}(t)\neq\overline{X}^{j}(t)\text{ for all }t\in[0,\infty),1\leq i,j\leq m,i\neq j)=1.

(9.2)

iii
For each
1\leq i\leq m
,

\displaystyle\overline{X}^{i}(t)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{b}(\overline{X}^{i}(u),\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{\overline{X}^{j}(u)})du,

(9.3)

\displaystyle\overline{X}^{i}(t)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}-\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\circ r_{t}\Big\},

(9.4)

where
r_{t}
is the time reversal operator defined before Lemma
8.1
.

Proof.

From Lemma
7.6
ii,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a quasi-regular, strongly local Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Hence, we obtain a
\mu^{[m]}
-symmetric diffusion
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
properly associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
by the general theory of Dirichlet forms
[
5
]
.

Because
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a quasi-regular Dirichlet form, we use the Fukushima decomposition (cf.
[
5
, Th. 4.2.6]
).
We regard the coordinate function
x^{i}
as a function on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}
in an obvious manner for
1\leq i\leq m
.
Then,
x^{i}\in\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
Although
x^{i}\notin\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}}
, we can apply the Fukushima decomposition to
x^{i}
by localization.

Consider the additive functional
A^{[x^{i}]}(t):=\overline{X}^{i}(t)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)
of
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
.
Let
\zeta
be the lifetime of the diffusion
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
.
Applying the Fukushima decomposition to
x^{i}
, we have a decomposition of
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
such that, for
1\leq i\leq m
,

\displaystyle\overline{X}^{i}(t\wedge\zeta)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)=M^{[x^{i}]}(t\wedge\zeta)+N^{[x^{i}]}(t\wedge\zeta),

(9.5)

where
M^{[x^{i}]}
is the martingale additive functional of finite energy and
N^{[x^{i}]}
is the continuous additive functional of zero energy
[
7
,
5
]
.
By a standard calculation (cf.
[
5
, pp.164-165]
), we can show that
M^{[x^{i}]}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, are
martingales such that

\displaystyle M^{[x^{i}]}(t\wedge\zeta)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i},

(9.6)

where
B^{i}
are independent
d
-dimensional Brownian motions.
For any
g\in\mathscr{D}_{\circ\mathrm{b}}^{[m]}
,

\displaystyle-\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}(x^{i},g)

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathsf{S}}\mathfrak{b}(s^{i},\sum_{j\neq i}^{m}\delta_{s^{j}}+\sum_{k>m}\delta_{s^{k}})g(\mathsf{s}^{[m]})\mu^{[m]}(d\mathsf{s}^{[m]}).

(9.7)

Here, we set
\mathsf{s}^{[m]}=(s^{1},\ldots,s^{m},\sum_{j>m}\delta_{s^{j}})
.
Using (
9.7
) together with localization and
[
7
, Th. 5.2.4]
, we have

\displaystyle N^{[x^{i}]}(t\wedge\zeta)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta}\mathfrak{b}(\overline{X}^{i}(u),\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{\overline{X}^{j}}(u))du.

(9.8)

Hence, from (
9.5
), (
9.6
), and (
9.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{X}^{i}(t\wedge\zeta)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta}\mathfrak{b}(\overline{X}^{i}(u),\sum_{j\neq i}^{\infty}\delta_{\overline{X}^{j}(u)})du.

(9.9)

Applying the Lyons–Zheng decomposition
[
5
, p.284]
to
x^{i}
, we obtain, from (
9.9
),

\displaystyle\overline{X}^{i}(t\wedge\zeta)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\{\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\circ r_{t}\Big\}.

(9.10)

Let
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}=(\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{m},\overline{\mathsf{X}}^{m*})
, where

\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{m}=(\overline{X}^{1},\ldots,\overline{X}^{m})
and
\overline{\mathsf{X}}^{m*}=\sum_{j>m}^{\infty}\delta_{\overline{X}^{j}}
.
Let

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{tp}}=\sup\{t\geq 0;\sup_{0\leq u\leq t}\lvert\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{u}^{m}\rvert<\infty\},

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{cnf}}=\sup\{t\geq 0;\sup_{0\leq u\leq t}\overline{\mathsf{X}}^{m*}_{u}(\overline{S}_{R})<\infty\text{ for all }R\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Then, we have
\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{tp}}\wedge\zeta_{\mathrm{cnf}}
.
Suppose
\zeta<\infty
.
Then, taking
t\to\infty
in (
9.10
) yields

\displaystyle\overline{X}^{i}(\zeta)-\overline{X}^{i}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\Big\{\int_{0}^{\zeta}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}-\int_{0}^{\zeta}\sigma(\overline{X}^{i}(u))dB_{u}^{i}\circ r_{t}\Big\}.

(9.11)

Note that the right-hand side of (
9.11
) is finite. This implies that
\overline{X}^{i}(\zeta)
is finite.
Because
\lim_{t\to\zeta}\overline{X}^{i}(t\wedge\zeta)=\overline{X}^{i}(\zeta)
,
X^{i}
does not explode at finite time
\zeta
.
Hence, we obtain
\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{cnf}}
.

Because
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu})
is a quasi-regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu)
, there exists an increasing sequence of compact sets
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{q}}
in
\mathsf{S}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu}(\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{q}}^{c})=0.

(9.12)

Let
\{a_{\mathsf{q}}\}_{\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of sequences
a_{\mathsf{q}}=\{a_{\mathsf{q}}(R)\}_{R\in\mathbb{N}}
of natural numbers satisfying

a_{\mathsf{q}}(R)<a_{\mathsf{q}}(R+1)
and
a_{\mathsf{q}}(R)<a_{\mathsf{q}+1}(R)
for all
\mathsf{q},R\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, from the property of compact sets of
\mathsf{S}

[
12
]
, there exists such an
\{a_{\mathsf{q}}\}_{\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{q}}\subset\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{q}}]:=\cap_{R=1}^{\infty}\{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{S};\mathsf{s}(S_{R})\leq a_{\mathsf{q}}(R)\}.

(9.13)

Hence, from (
9.12
) and (
9.13
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu}((\cup_{\mathsf{q}=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{q}}])^{c})=0.

(9.14)

Using (
9.14
) and Lemma
9.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu^{[m]}}((\mathfrak{u}^{[m,0]})^{-1}(\cup_{\mathsf{q}=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{q}}])^{c})=0.

Hence, using the general theory of Dirichlet forms
[
7
,
5
]
, we obtain

\displaystyle P^{\mu^{[m]}}(\overline{\mathsf{X}}_{t}^{m*}\in(\mathfrak{u}^{[m,0]})^{-1}(\cup_{\mathsf{q}=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{K}[a_{\mathsf{q}}])^{c}\text{ for some }0\leq t<\infty)=0.

From this, we deduce
\zeta_{\mathrm{cnf}}=\infty
,
P^{\mu^{[m]}}
-a.s.
We have already proved that
\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{cnf}}
. Therefore,
\zeta=\infty
,
P^{\mu^{[m]}}
-a.s.
Thus
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
is conservative, which implies i.

We obtain (
9.1
) from
\zeta_{\mathrm{tp}}=\zeta=\infty
.
Because
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
is
\mu^{[m]}
-symmetric and conservative from i, we have, for all
R,T\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle E\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\mathfrak{b}(\overline{X}_{u}^{i},\overline{\mathsf{X}}_{u}^{i\diamond})\rvert^{2}du;\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{0}^{i},\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{0}^{j}\in S_{R}\Big]\leq c_{\small\ref{;14}}T\int_{S_{R}\times\mathsf{S}}\lvert\mathfrak{b}({x},\mathsf{s})\rvert^{2}d\mu^{[1]}<\infty.

(9.15)

From (
9.15
) and Theorem
6.3
, we obtain (
9.2
). We have thus proved ii.

Because
\zeta=\infty
, (
9.3
) and (
9.4
) follow from (
9.9
) and (
9.10
). This yields iii.
∎

Remark 9.1
.

In the proof of Theorem
9.1
, we have applied the Lyons-Zheng decomposition to the limit process
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
directly because the upper Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is quasi-regular on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
according to Lemma
7.6
ii.
However, we have not yet proved that the lower Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\underline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
is quasi-regular at this stage. Hence, in the proof of Theorem
8.1
, we applied the Lyons-Zheng decomposition to the finite-volume Dirichlet forms
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}}_{R},\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{R}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
, which is known to be quasi-regular from Lemma
7.2
.

9.2.
Weak solutions of ISDEs for the upper Dirichlet form and the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

In Subsection
9.1
, we have constructed the
m
-labeled process
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
.
The construction of the fully labeled process
\overline{\mathbf{X}}
for these partially labeled processes have not yet done.
The goal of the subsection is to construct the fully labeled process
\overline{\mathbf{X}}
and prove that it satisfies ISDE (
2.41
) (Theorem
9.2
) and complete the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

The proof in the present section is different from that of Subsection
8.2
.
The upper Dirichlet form Dirichlet form
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
is quasi-regular.
Hence, we will utilize the Dirichlet form theory and the general theorems developed in
[
24
,
25
]
.

For a label
\mathfrak{l}=(\mathfrak{l}^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathfrak{l}^{[0]}(\mathsf{s})=\mathsf{s}
and

\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{s})=(\mathfrak{l}^{1}(\mathsf{s}),\ldots,\mathfrak{l}^{m}(\mathsf{s}),\sum_{j>m}\delta_{\mathfrak{l}^{j}(\mathsf{s})})
.
Let
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}\!:\!W_{\mathrm{NE}}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s,i}})\!\to\!C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
be as in Lemma
13.2
. We define
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]}(\mathsf{w})=(\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{1}(\mathsf{w}),\ldots,\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{m}(\mathsf{w}),\sum_{j>m}^{\infty}\delta_{\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{j}(\mathsf{w})}).

Here, for a labeled path
\mathbf{w}=(w^{i})
, we define the measure-valued path
\mathsf{w}=\sum_{i}\delta_{w^{i}}

by
\mathsf{w}_{t}=\sum_{i}\delta_{w_{t}^{i}}
.
From Lemma
13.2
,
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}(\mathsf{w})_{0}=\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{w}_{0})
. Hence,
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]}(\mathsf{w})_{0}=\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{w}_{0})
.

Let
\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{s})}^{\mu^{[m]}}
be the distribution of the diffusion
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
staring at
\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{s})

properly associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
Note that, from Lemma
7.6
,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a quasi-regular, strongly local symmetric Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

Lemma 9.2

(
[
24
, Th. 2.4]
)
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. For
\mu
-a.s.
\mathsf{s}
and each
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{s})}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\overline{P}^{\mu}_{\mathsf{s}}\circ(\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]})^{-1}.

(9.16)

Proof.

From Lemma
7.4
ii,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
From Lemma
7.5
ii,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is the closure of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[m]}})
on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.
From Lemma
7.6
ii,
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
is a quasi-regular, strongly local symmetric Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(\mu^{[m]})
.

According to Theorem
9.1
, none of the labeled particles
\overline{X}^{i}
, where
i=1,\ldots,m
, in
\mathbf{X}^{[m]}
explode or collide with other labeled particles.
Hence, from (2.17) in Theorem 2.4 in
[
24
]
, we have (
9.16
).
∎

Remark 9.2
.

We provide a brief explanation of the difference between
[
24
, Th. 2.4]
and Lemma
9.2
.
In
[
24
, Th. 2.4]
, the following were assumed:

i
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[k]}},\mathscr{D}_{\circ}^{\mu^{[k]}})
is closable on
L^{2}(\mu^{[k]})
for
k=0
and
m
.

ii The
m
-point correlation function of
\mu
is a locally bounded for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

iii
\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s}}^{c})=0
.

iv each tagged particle of
\overline{\mathsf{X}}
does not explode.

In the present paper, i follows from Lemma
7.5
.

In
[
24
, Th. 2.4]
, ii was used only for proving the quasi-regularity of
(\mathscr{E}^{\mu^{[m]}},\overline{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu^{[m]}})
.
Thus, we can replace ii by the quasi-regularity of the Dirichlet forms in Lemma
7.6
.

Instead of iii, non-collision of the labeled particles is sufficient.
Indeed, in
[
24
]
,
\mathrm{Cap}^{\mu}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{s}}^{c})=0
was used to prove the non-collision of the tagged particles
\{\overline{X}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}
through
[
24
, Lem. 4.1]
. In the proof of Lemma
9.2
, the non-collision of the labeled particles
\overline{X}^{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, is sufficient and, thus, Theorem
9.1
works well.

Instead of iv, the non-explosion of the labeled particles
\overline{X}^{i}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, is sufficient because (
9.16
) is the property of the
m
-labeled process

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{[m]}=(\overline{X}^{1},\ldots,\overline{X}^{m},\sum_{i>m}\delta_{\overline{X}^{i}}).

Thus, we can prove Lemma
9.2
using the proof of
[
24
, Th. 2.4]
.

For
\mathbf{x}=(x^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
and
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, we define the function
\mathfrak{u}^{[m]}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}^{[m]}(\mathbf{x})=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{m},\sum_{i>m}^{\infty}\delta_{x^{i}}),\ m\geq 1,\quad\mathfrak{u}^{[0]}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{u}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\delta_{x^{i}}.

For
\mathbf{w}\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
and
m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]}(\mathbf{w})
by
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]}(\mathbf{w})(t)=\mathfrak{u}^{[m]}(\mathbf{w}(t))
.

Theorem 9.2
.

Assume
(
A1
)
–
(
A3
)
. Then the following hold.

i

There exists a family of probability measures
\overline{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbb{N}}
on

C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{l}^{[m]}(\mathsf{s})}^{\mu^{[m]}}=\overline{P}_{\mathfrak{l}(\mathsf{s})}^{\mathbb{N}}\circ(\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{path}}^{[m]})^{-1}\quad\text{ for $\mu$-a.s.\,$\mathsf{s}$, $m\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}$}.

(9.17)

ii

Let
\overline{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{w}
. Then,
